```java
import java.util.concurrent.locks.*;

public class BoundedBuffer {
    private final Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private final Condition notFull = lock.newCondition();
    private final Condition notEmpty = lock.newCondition();
    
    private final Object[] buffer;
    private int count = 0;
    private int putIndex = 0;
    private int takeIndex = 0;
    
    public BoundedBuffer(int capacity) {
        buffer = new Object[capacity];
    }
    
    public void put(Object item) throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (count == buffer.length) {
                notFull.await();
            }
            
            buffer[putIndex] = item;
            putIndex = (putIndex + 1) % buffer.length;
            count++;
            
            notEmpty.signal();
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
    
    public Object take() throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (count == 0) {
                notEmpty.await();
            }
            
            Object item = buffer[takeIndex];
            takeIndex = (takeIndex + 1) % buffer.length;
            count--;
            
            notFull.signal();
            
            return item;
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
}
```

**When to Use Monitor Object Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When you have shared data that needs mutual exclusion.

2. When threads need to wait for specific conditions.

3. When you want to encapsulate synchronization logic with the data.

4. In classic producer-consumer problems.

5. When building thread-safe data structures.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Bounded Buffers:** Producer-consumer queues
- **Thread Pools:** Workers wait for tasks
- **Connection Pools:** Threads wait for available connections
- **Blocking Queues:** Java's BlockingQueue implementations
- **Resource Managers:** Managing limited resources

**Advantages of Monitor Object Pattern**

1. **Encapsulation:** Synchronization logic is inside the object.

2. **Simplicity:** Clean separation of concerns.

3. **Language Support:** Built into many languages (Java, C#).

4. **Familiar Pattern:** Well-understood by developers.

5. **Condition Management:** Explicit wait/notify for conditions.

**Disadvantages of Monitor Object Pattern**

1. **Single Lock:** All methods synchronized on same lock (can limit concurrency).

2. **Potential for Deadlock:** Can still deadlock if not careful.

3. **Nested Monitor Problem:** Can cause issues with nested monitors.

4. **Spurious Wakeups:** wait() can return without signal (must check in loop).

5. **Not Reentrant by Default:** Some implementations may not be reentrant.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Monitor | Mutual exclusion + condition | Complete synchronization unit |
| Mutex | Mutual exclusion only | No condition waiting |
| Semaphore | Counting permits | Can be used for both |
| Guarded Suspension | Wait for condition | Often implemented with monitor |

**Monitor Object vs Active Object**

- **Monitor:** Methods execute in caller's thread with mutual exclusion
- **Active Object:** Methods execute in different thread, returns futures

**Monitor Object vs Read-Write Lock**

- **Monitor:** Single lock for all methods
- **Read-Write Lock:** Different locks for read/write

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Not Checking Condition in Loop:** Must use while, not if.

2. **Signaling While Not Holding Lock:** Must hold lock when signaling.

3. **Nested Monitor Deadlock:** Monitor inside monitor can cause deadlock.

4. **Lost Wakeup:** Signaling before thread waits.

5. **Not Handling InterruptedException:** Threads may be interrupted.

**Best Practices**

1. **Always Use While Loop:** while (!condition) wait();

2. **Signal After State Change:** Only signal when condition actually changed.

3. **Minimize Work in Synchronized Block:** Release lock quickly.

4. **Use notifyAll() Unless Sure:** Prevents missed signals.

5. **Document Waiting Conditions:** Make clear what threads wait for.

---

[Page 14]

## Chapter 12: Guarded Suspension Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Guarded Suspension pattern manages operations that must wait until a certain condition is true. Threads suspend execution (wait) until the condition becomes true, at which point they are notified and continue.

**Understanding the Problem**

In concurrent systems, operations often depend on conditions that may not be immediately true:

- A consumer cannot consume from an empty queue
- A client cannot get a connection from an empty pool
- A thread cannot proceed until some initialization is complete

The naive approach is busy-waiting (polling), which wastes CPU cycles. A better approach is to suspend the thread until the condition becomes true.

**The Solution**

The Guarded Suspension pattern provides a mechanism for threads to:

1. Check a condition (the guard)
2. If false, suspend (wait) until notified
3. When notified, recheck condition (in case of spurious wakeup)
4. When true, proceed with operation

**Key Concepts**

- **Guard:** The condition that must be true to proceed
- **Suspension:** Thread waits efficiently (doesn't consume CPU)
- **Notification:** Another thread signals that condition may be true
- **Spurious Wakeup:** Thread may wake without notification (must recheck)

**Diagram: Guarded Suspension**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   GUARDED SUSPENSION                          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              CONSUMER THREAD FLOW                      │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   acquire lock   │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ▼                                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │  check guard     │                               │    │
│  │  │  (queue empty?)  │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ├─────True─────┐                          │    │
│  │           │              │                          │    │
│  │           │ False        │                          │    │
│  │           ▼              │                          │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐    │                          │    │
│  │  │     wait()       │    │                          │    │
│  │  │  (release lock,  │    │                          │    │
│  │  │   suspend)       │    │                          │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘    │                          │    │
│  │           │              │                          │    │
│  │           │ (wake up)    │                          │    │
│  │           │              │                          │    │
│  │           └──────────────┘                          │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           │ (reacquire lock)                        │    │
│  │           ▼                                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │  recheck guard   │                               │    │
│  │  │  (queue still    │                               │    │
│  │  │   empty?)        │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ├─────True─────┐                          │    │
│  │           │              │ (spurious wakeup)       │    │
│  │           │ False        │                          │    │
│  │           ▼              │                          │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐    │                          │    │
│  │  │  perform action  │    │                          │    │
│  │  │  (consume item)  │    │                          │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘    │                          │    │
│  │           │              │                          │    │
│  │           ▼              │                          │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐    │                          │    │
│  │  │   release lock   │    │                          │    │
│  │  └──────────────────┘    │                          │    │
│  │                          │                          │    │
│  └──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┘    │
│                             │                               │
│  ┌──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┐    │
│  │           PRODUCER THREAD │                          │    │
│  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                          ▼                          │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   acquire lock   │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ▼                                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │  add item to    │                               │    │
│  │  │     queue       │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ▼                                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │  notify() - wake │                               │    │
│  │  │  waiting threads │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬─────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                         │    │
│  │           ▼                                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   release lock   │                               │    │
│  │  └──────────────────┘                               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
public class GuardedQueue<T> {
    private final Queue<T> queue = new LinkedList<>();
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    
    public T take() throws InterruptedException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            // Guarded suspension: wait while queue empty
            while (queue.isEmpty()) {
                lock.wait(); // suspend until notified
            }
            return queue.poll();
        }
    }
    
    public void put(T item) {
        synchronized(lock) {
            queue.add(item);
            lock.notify(); // wake one waiting thread
        }
    }
}
```

**Generic Guarded Suspension Pattern**

```java
public abstract class GuardedBlock<T> {
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    private volatile boolean condition = false;
    private T result;
    
    public T execute() throws InterruptedException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            while (!conditionMet()) {
                lock.wait();
            }
            return getResult();
        }
    }
    
    public void signal(T result) {
        synchronized(lock) {
            this.result = result;
            this.condition = true;
            lock.notifyAll();
        }
    }
    
    protected abstract boolean conditionMet();
    protected T getResult() { return result; }
}
```

**When to Use Guarded Suspension Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When threads need to wait for conditions to become true.

2. When busy-waiting (polling) is inefficient.

3. In producer-consumer scenarios.

4. When implementing resource pools.

5. When threads have dependencies on each other's state.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Blocking Queue:** Consumer waits for items
- **Connection Pool:** Client waits for available connection
- **Initialization:** Thread waits for service to be ready
- **Lazy Loading:** Wait until data is loaded
- **Pipeline Stages:** Wait for previous stage to complete

**Advantages of Guarded Suspension Pattern**

1. **Efficient:** Threads sleep, not busy-wait.

2. **Simple:** Clean separation of condition checking.

3. **Language Support:** Built into many languages.

4. **Decoupled:** Producers and consumers don't need to know each other.

5. **Flexible:** Can wait on any condition.

**Disadvantages of Guarded Suspension Pattern**

1. **Spurious Wakeups:** Must check condition in loop.

2. **Lost Wakeups:** Can happen if signal before wait.

3. **Deadlock Risk:** Can deadlock if conditions never met.

4. **Debugging Difficulty:** Hard to trace wait/notify chains.

5. **Not Reusable:** Each condition needs its own guard.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Guarded Suspension | Wait for condition | General pattern |
| Monitor Object | Mutual exclusion + condition | Specific implementation |
| Balking | Abort if condition not met | Opposite (don't wait) |
| Semaphore | Counting permits | Can implement guarding |

**Guarded Suspension vs Balking**

- **Guarded Suspension:** Wait until condition is true
- **Balking:** Return immediately if condition not true

**Guarded Suspension vs Producer-Consumer**

Guarded Suspension is a key part of the Producer-Consumer pattern. Producer-Consumer is a higher-level pattern that uses guarded suspension.

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Using if Instead of while:** Must use while for condition check.

2. **Notifying Outside Lock:** Must hold lock when notifying.

3. **Lost Wakeup:** Signaling before thread starts waiting.

4. **Spurious Wakeups:** Not rechecking condition.

5. **Deadlock:** If condition never becomes true.

**Best Practices**

1. **Always Use While Loop:** while (!condition) wait();

2. **Hold Lock When Checking Condition:** Condition must be checked under lock.

3. **Hold Lock When Notifying:** Notify must be inside synchronized block.

4. **Use notifyAll() Unless Sure:** Prevents lost wakeups.

5. **Document Guard Conditions:** Make clear what threads are waiting for.

6. **Consider Timeouts:** Use timed wait to avoid waiting forever.

# PART THREE: TASK DISTRIBUTION PATTERNS

## Chapter 13: Thread Pool Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Thread Pool pattern maintains a pool of worker threads that can execute tasks concurrently. Instead of creating a new thread for each task, tasks are submitted to a queue, and idle worker threads pick them up and execute them.

**Understanding the Problem**

Creating a new thread for each task has several drawbacks:

1. **Overhead:** Thread creation and destruction are expensive operations.

2. **Resource Limits:** The OS has limits on the number of threads.

3. **Unbounded Growth:** Under load, you might create thousands of threads, overwhelming the system.

4. **Performance:** Too many threads cause context switching overhead.

5. **Latency:** Creating a thread adds latency before task execution.

**The Solution**

The Thread Pool pattern pre-creates a fixed number of threads that wait for work. Tasks are submitted to a queue. Idle threads take tasks from the queue and execute them. After completing a task, the thread returns to the pool to wait for the next task.

**Key Concepts**

- **Thread Pool:** Collection of worker threads
- **Task Queue:** Queue of pending tasks
- **Worker Thread:** Thread that takes tasks from queue and executes them
- **Core Pool Size:** Minimum number of threads to keep alive
- **Maximum Pool Size:** Maximum number of threads allowed
- **Keep-Alive Time:** How long idle threads survive

**Diagram: Thread Pool**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      THREAD POOL PATTERN                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                  TASK SUBMISSION                       │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │    │
│  │  │ Client 1 │  │ Client 2 │  │ Client 3 │          │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘          │    │
│  │       │             │             │                 │    │
│  │       │ submit()    │ submit()    │ submit()        │    │
│  │       ▼             ▼             ▼                 │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │               TASK QUEUE                       │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐     │   │    │
│  │  │  │Task1│ │Task2│ │Task3│ │Task4│ │Task5│     │   │    │
│  │  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘     │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            │ dequeue                 │    │
│  │                            ▼                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │               THREAD POOL                       │   │    │

│  │  │  │ Worker 1 │  │ Worker 2 │  │ Worker 3 │     │   │    │
│  │  │  │  (idle)  │  │ (busy)   │  │ (busy)   │     │   │    │
│  │  │  └──────────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘     │   │    │
│  │  │                     │              │           │   │    │
│  │  │                     ▼              ▼           │   │    │
│  │  │              ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐     │   │    │
│  │  │              │ Task 2   │    │ Task 3   │     │   │    │
│  │  │              │executing│    │executing│     │   │    │
│  │  │              └──────────┘    └──────────┘     │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │           THREAD POOL CONFIGURATION                    │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Java ThreadPoolExecutor                          │    │
│  │  ThreadPoolExecutor executor = new ThreadPoolExecutor(│    │
│  │      5, // core pool size                            │    │
│  │      10, // maximum pool size                        │    │
│  │      60, // keep-alive time                          │    │
│  │      TimeUnit.SECONDS,                               │    │
│  │      new LinkedBlockingQueue<Runnable>()             │    │
│  │  );                                                  │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Submit task                                      │    │
│  │  executor.submit(() -> {                             │    │
│  │      // task code                                     │    │
│  │  });                                                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class ThreadPoolExample {
    private final ExecutorService threadPool;
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> taskQueue;
    
    public ThreadPoolExample(int poolSize, int queueSize) {
        this.taskQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueSize);
        this.threadPool = new ThreadPoolExecutor(
            poolSize,                     // core pool size
            poolSize,                     // max pool size
            60L, TimeUnit.SECONDS,        // keep alive time
            taskQueue,                     // work queue
            new ThreadPoolExecutor.AbortPolicy() // rejection policy
        );
    }
    
    public void submitTask(Runnable task) {
        threadPool.submit(task);
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        threadPool.shutdown();
        try {
            if (!threadPool.awaitTermination(60, TimeUnit.SECONDS)) {
                threadPool.shutdownNow();
            }
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            threadPool.shutdownNow();
        }
    }
}
```

**Custom Simple Thread Pool**

```java
public class SimpleThreadPool {
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> taskQueue;
    private final List<Worker> workers;
    private volatile boolean isRunning = true;
    
    public SimpleThreadPool(int numThreads, int queueSize) {
        taskQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueSize);
        workers = new ArrayList<>();
        
        for (int i = 0; i < numThreads; i++) {
            Worker worker = new Worker();
            workers.add(worker);
            worker.start();
        }
    }
    
    public void submit(Runnable task) throws InterruptedException {
        if (!isRunning) {
            throw new RejectedExecutionException("Pool is shutdown");
        }
        taskQueue.put(task);
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        isRunning = false;
        for (Worker worker : workers) {
            worker.interrupt();
        }
    }
    
    private class Worker extends Thread {
        public void run() {
            while (isRunning) {
                try {
                    Runnable task = taskQueue.take(); // blocks if empty
                    task.run();
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                    break;
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

**Thread Pool Sizing**

The optimal pool size depends on the workload:

**CPU-Bound Tasks:**
- Pool size ≈ Number of CPU cores
- More threads cause context switching overhead

**I/O-Bound Tasks:**
- Pool size can be larger (threads spend time waiting)
- Formula: cores × (1 + wait time / service time)

**Mixed Workloads:**
- Consider separate pools for different task types

**Rejection Policies**

When the queue is full and all threads are busy, new tasks must be rejected:

1. **AbortPolicy:** Throws RejectedExecutionException
2. **CallerRunsPolicy:** Executes task in caller's thread
3. **DiscardPolicy:** Silently discards task
4. **DiscardOldestPolicy:** Discards oldest task in queue

**When to Use Thread Pool Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When you have many short-lived tasks.

2. When you want to limit resource usage.

3. In server applications handling many requests.

4. When thread creation overhead is significant.

5. When you need to control concurrency level.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Web Servers:** Handle HTTP requests
- **Database Connection Pools:** Manage connections
- **Background Job Processors:** Process queued jobs
- **Event Handlers:** Process events asynchronously
- **Parallel Processing:** Process data in parallel

**Advantages of Thread Pool Pattern**

1. **Reduced Overhead:** Threads are reused, not created per task.

2. **Resource Control:** Limits on threads prevent resource exhaustion.

3. **Improved Responsiveness:** No thread creation latency.

4. **Load Leveling:** Queue smooths out bursty loads.

5. **Thread Management:** Pool handles thread lifecycle.

6. **Monitoring:** Can track queue size, active threads.

**Disadvantages of Thread Pool Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** More complex than creating threads on demand.

2. **Tuning Difficulty:** Right pool size is hard to determine.

3. **Queue Bounds:** Need to decide queue size and rejection policy.

4. **Deadlock Risk:** If tasks depend on each other, pool can deadlock.

5. **Thread Leak:** If tasks don't complete, threads may leak.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Thread Pool | Reuse threads | Fixed set of workers |
| Worker Pool | Similar | Often used interchangeably |
| Fork-Join | Divide and conquer | Specialized for recursive tasks |
| Active Object | Decouple invocation | Method execution in different thread |

**Thread Pool vs Fork-Join Pool**

- **Thread Pool:** General-purpose, any tasks
- **Fork-Join Pool:** Specialized for divide-and-conquer, work stealing

**Thread Pool vs Worker Pool**

Often used interchangeably, but "worker pool" sometimes implies heterogeneous workers.

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Pool Too Small:** Tasks queue up, response time suffers.

2. **Pool Too Large:** Too many threads cause thrashing.

3. **No Rejection Policy:** Tasks may be lost.

4. **Not Shutting Down:** Threads keep running, prevent JVM exit.

5. **Task Dependencies:** Tasks waiting for other tasks can deadlock pool.

**Best Practices**

1. **Size Pool Appropriately:** Consider task type (CPU vs I/O).

2. **Use Named Threads:** Helps with debugging.

3. **Handle Rejection:** Always have a rejection policy.

4. **Monitor Pool Metrics:** Queue size, active threads, completed tasks.

5. **Shutdown Gracefully:** Allow queued tasks to complete.

6. **Consider Work Stealing:** For recursive tasks, use ForkJoinPool.

7. **Don't Submit Tasks That Wait:** Can cause pool exhaustion.

---

[Page 16]

## Chapter 14: Worker Pool Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Worker Pool pattern is similar to Thread Pool but often implies a pool of identical workers that process tasks from a queue. Workers can be threads, processes, or even separate machines.

**Understanding the Problem**

In distributed and parallel systems, you often have a set of identical workers that can process tasks independently. You need a way to:

1. Distribute tasks among workers
2. Balance load across workers
3. Handle worker failures
4. Scale the number of workers dynamically

**The Solution**

The Worker Pool pattern creates multiple identical workers that pull tasks from a shared queue. Each worker processes one task at a time and returns to the pool when done.

**Key Concepts**

- **Task Queue:** Central queue of pending tasks
- **Workers:** Identical processing units
- **Dispatcher:** Optional component that assigns tasks (vs. workers pulling)
- **Result Queue:** Optional queue for collecting results

**Diagram: Worker Pool**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      WORKER POOL PATTERN                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                    TASK SOURCES                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │    │
│  │  │ Client 1 │  │ Client 2 │  │ Client 3 │          │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘          │    │
│  │       │             │             │                 │    │
│  │       └─────────────┼─────────────┘                 │    │
│  │                     │                               │    │
│  │                     ▼                               │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │               TASK QUEUE                       │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐     │   │    │
│  │  │  │Task1│ │Task2│ │Task3│ │Task4│ │Task5│     │   │    │
│  │  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘     │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            │ workers pull tasks      │    │
│  │                            ▼                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │               WORKER POOL                       │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐     │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Worker 1 │  │ Worker 2 │  │ Worker 3 │     │   │    │
│  │  │  │(process  │  │(process  │  │(process  │     │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Task1)   │  │ Task2)   │  │ Task3)   │     │   │    │
│  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘     │   │    │
│  │  │                                                 │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐     │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Worker 4 │  │ Worker 5 │  │ Worker 6 │     │   │    │
│  │  │  │ (idle)   │  │ (idle)   │  │ (idle)   │     │   │    │
│  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘     │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            ▼                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │              RESULT QUEUE (optional)           │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐                     │   │    │
│  │  │  │Res1 │ │Res2 │ │Res3 │                     │   │    │
│  │  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘                     │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            │                         │    │
│  │                            ▼                         │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │              RESULT CONSUMER                    │   │    │
│  │  │            (processes results)                 │   │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
public class WorkerPool<T, R> {
    private final BlockingQueue<T> taskQueue;
    private final BlockingQueue<R> resultQueue;
    private final List<Worker> workers;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    public WorkerPool(int numWorkers, int queueSize) {
        this.taskQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueSize);
        this.resultQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        this.workers = new ArrayList<>();
        
        for (int i = 0; i < numWorkers; i++) {
            Worker worker = new Worker();
            workers.add(worker);
            worker.start();
        }
    }
    
    public void submitTask(T task) throws InterruptedException {
        taskQueue.put(task);
    }
    
    public R takeResult() throws InterruptedException {
        return resultQueue.take();
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        for (Worker worker : workers) {
            worker.interrupt();
        }
    }
    
    private class Worker extends Thread {
        public void run() {
            while (running) {
                try {
                    T task = taskQueue.take();
                    R result = processTask(task);
                    resultQueue.put(result);
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                    break;
                }
            }
        }
        
        private R processTask(T task) {
            // Override in subclass or provide processor
            return null;
        }
    }
}
```

**Dynamic Worker Pool**

```java
public class DynamicWorkerPool<T> {
    private final BlockingQueue<T> taskQueue;
    private final List<Worker> workers = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>();
    private final int minWorkers;
    private final int maxWorkers;
    private final int queueThreshold;
    
    public DynamicWorkerPool(int min, int max, int threshold) {
        this.minWorkers = min;
        this.maxWorkers = max;
        this.queueThreshold = threshold;
        this.taskQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        
        for (int i = 0; i < min; i++) {
            addWorker();
        }
        
        startMonitor();
    }
    
    private void addWorker() {
        Worker w = new Worker();
        workers.add(w);
        w.start();
    }
    
    private void startMonitor() {
        Thread monitor = new Thread(() -> {
            while (true) {
                int queueSize = taskQueue.size();
                int workerCount = workers.size();
                
                if (queueSize > queueThreshold && workerCount < maxWorkers) {
                    addWorker();
                } else if (queueSize == 0 && workerCount > minWorkers) {
                    removeWorker();
                }
                
                try {
                    Thread.sleep(1000);
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    break;
                }
            }
        });
        monitor.setDaemon(true);
        monitor.start();
    }
    
    private class Worker extends Thread {
        // Worker implementation
    }
}
```

**When to Use Worker Pool Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When you have many independent tasks that can be processed in parallel.

2. When you need to scale processing horizontally.

3. In batch processing systems.

4. When tasks are CPU-intensive and benefit from parallelism.

5. When you need to process tasks from multiple sources.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Image Processing:** Multiple workers process different images
- **Web Crawling:** Workers crawl different URLs
- **Data Transformation:** ETL pipelines
- **Report Generation:** Generate reports in parallel
- **Email Sending:** Send bulk emails

**Advantages of Worker Pool Pattern**

1. **Parallel Processing:** Multiple workers increase throughput.

2. **Scalability:** Can add more workers as load increases.

3. **Fault Tolerance:** Workers can fail independently.

4. **Load Balancing:** Natural load balancing as workers pull tasks.

5. **Backpressure:** Queue prevents overload.

**Disadvantages of Worker Pool Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** More complex than single-threaded processing.

2. **Resource Management:** Need to manage worker lifecycle.

3. **Result Collection:** Need to collect and combine results.

4. **Failure Handling:** Need to handle worker failures.

5. **Ordering:** Tasks may complete out of order.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Worker Pool | Parallel task processing | General pattern |
| Thread Pool | Reuse threads | Specific implementation |
| MapReduce | Large-scale data processing | Includes reduce phase |
| Fork-Join | Recursive decomposition | Work stealing |

**Worker Pool vs Thread Pool**

Often used interchangeably, but "worker pool" can refer to processes or machines, not just threads.

**Worker Pool vs MapReduce**

- **Worker Pool:** General parallel processing
- **MapReduce:** Specialized for large datasets with map and reduce phases

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Task Dependencies:** If tasks depend on each other, workers may deadlock.

2. **Resource Contention:** Workers competing for resources.

3. **Result Ordering:** Assuming results come back in order.

4. **Poison Tasks:** Tasks that never complete.

5. **Queue Overflow:** If producers outpace workers.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Bounded Queues:** Prevent memory exhaustion.

2. **Implement Backpressure:** Slow down producers when queue is full.

3. **Handle Worker Failures:** Restart failed workers.

4. **Monitor Queue Size:** Alert if queue grows too large.

5. **Consider Task Priority:** Use priority queue if needed.

6. **Implement Graceful Shutdown:** Allow in-flight tasks to complete.

7. **Use Timeouts:** Prevent workers from hanging forever.

---

[Page 17]

## Chapter 15: Active Object Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Active Object pattern decouples method invocation from method execution. Each object has its own thread of control and a queue of pending requests. Methods called on the active object return immediately (with a future), and execution happens asynchronously in the object's thread.

**Understanding the Problem**

In traditional object-oriented programming, when you call a method, the caller's thread executes the method. This has several limitations:

1. **Blocking:** The caller blocks until the method completes.

2. **Thread Safety:** You need synchronization if multiple threads call methods.

3. **Responsiveness:** Long-running methods freeze the caller.

4. **Concurrency Control:** Hard to manage concurrent access.

**The Solution**

The Active Object pattern creates objects with their own threads. Each active object has:

1. **Proxy:** The interface clients call (returns future immediately)
2. **Method Request:** Encapsulates a method call and its arguments
3. **Activation Queue:** Queue of pending method requests
4. **Scheduler:** Decides which request to execute next
5. **Servant:** The actual implementation of the methods
6. **Future:** Represents the pending result

**Diagram: Active Object**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     ACTIVE OBJECT PATTERN                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌──────────┐                                                │
│  │ Client   │                                                │
│  └────┬─────┘                                                │
│       │                                                      │
│       │ method call                                          │
│       │                                                      │
│       ▼                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                    PROXY                               │    │
│  │  (immediately returns future)                         │    │
│  └─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        │ creates method request             │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              ACTIVATION QUEUE                          │    │
│  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐                    │    │
│  │  │Req1 │ │Req2 │ │Req3 │ │Req4 │                    │    │
│  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘                    │    │
│  └─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        │ scheduler picks next request       │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                   SCHEDULER                            │    │
│  │  (runs in active object's thread)                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        │ dispatches to servant              │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                    SERVANT                              │    │
│  │  (actual method implementation)                        │    │
│  └─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        │ sets result                        │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                     FUTURE                              │    │
│  │  (initially empty, later contains result)             │    │
│  └─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        │ future.get() returns result        │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌──────────┐                                               │
│  │ Client   │                                               │
│  └──────────┘                                               │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 SEQUENCE DIAGRAM                       │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Client    Proxy     Queue    Scheduler   Servant   │    │
│  │    │        │         │          │          │       │    │
│  │    │──call()─
│    │        │         │          │          │       │    │
│  │    │        │──enqueue─>│          │          │       │    │
│  │    │<─future─│         │          │          │       │    │
│  │    │        │         │          │          │       │    │
│  │    │        │         │<─dequeue──│          │       │    │
│  │    │        │         │          │──dispatch─>│       │    │
│  │    │        │         │          │          │──exec─>│    │
│  │    │        │         │          │          │<─result─│    │
│  │    │        │         │<─complete─│          │       │    │
│  │    │──get()─│─────────│──────────│──────────│───────>│    │
│  │    │<─result─│         │          │          │       │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.*;

// 1. The Future/Result
class ActiveFuture<T> {
    private T result;
    private boolean completed = false;
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    
    public T get() throws InterruptedException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            while (!completed) {
                lock.wait();
            }
            return result;
        }
    }
    
    public void set(T result) {
        synchronized(lock) {
            this.result = result;
            this.completed = true;
            lock.notifyAll();
        }
    }
}

// 2. Method Request
abstract class MethodRequest<T> {
    protected final ActiveFuture<T> future;
    
    public MethodRequest(ActiveFuture<T> future) {
        this.future = future;
    }
    
    public abstract void execute();
}

// 3. Concrete Method Request
class PrintRequest extends MethodRequest<String> {
    private final String message;
    private final Servant servant;
    
    public PrintRequest(String message, Servant servant, ActiveFuture<String> future) {
        super(future);
        this.message = message;
        this.servant = servant;
    }
    
    @Override
    public void execute() {
        String result = servant.print(message);
        future.set(result);
    }
}

class CalculateRequest extends MethodRequest<Integer> {
    private final int a;
    private final int b;
    private final Servant servant;
    
    public CalculateRequest(int a, int b, Servant servant, ActiveFuture<Integer> future) {
        super(future);
        this.a = a;
        this.b = b;
        this.servant = servant;
    }
    
    @Override
    public void execute() {
        int result = servant.calculate(a, b);
        future.set(result);
    }
}

// 4. Servant (actual implementation)
class Servant {
    public String print(String message) {
        try {
            Thread.sleep(1000); // Simulate work
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
        }
        return "Printed: " + message;
    }
    
    public int calculate(int a, int b) {
        try {
            Thread.sleep(500); // Simulate work
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
        }
        return a + b;
    }
}

// 5. Scheduler and Activation Queue
class Scheduler implements Runnable {
    private final BlockingQueue<MethodRequest<?>> queue;
    private final Servant servant;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    public Scheduler(Servant servant) {
        this.queue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        this.servant = servant;
    }
    
    public <T> ActiveFuture<T> enqueue(MethodRequest<T> request) {
        queue.offer(request);
        return request.future;
    }
    
    @Override
    public void run() {
        while (running) {
            try {
                MethodRequest<?> request = queue.take();
                request.execute();
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                break;
            }
        }
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
    }
}

// 6. Proxy (Active Object)
class ActiveObject {
    private final Scheduler scheduler;
    private final Thread schedulerThread;
    
    public ActiveObject() {
        Servant servant = new Servant();
        this.scheduler = new Scheduler(servant);
        this.schedulerThread = new Thread(scheduler);
        this.schedulerThread.start();
    }
    
    public ActiveFuture<String> print(String message) {
        ActiveFuture<String> future = new ActiveFuture<>();
        PrintRequest request = new PrintRequest(message, 
            new Servant(), future); // In real impl, share servant
        return scheduler.enqueue(request);
    }
    
    public ActiveFuture<Integer> calculate(int a, int b) {
        ActiveFuture<Integer> future = new ActiveFuture<>();
        CalculateRequest request = new CalculateRequest(a, b, 
            new Servant(), future);
        return scheduler.enqueue(request);
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        scheduler.shutdown();
        schedulerThread.interrupt();
    }
}

// 7. Client Usage
public class ActiveObjectDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        ActiveObject activeObject = new ActiveObject();
        
        // Non-blocking calls
        ActiveFuture<String> future1 = activeObject.print("Hello");
        ActiveFuture<Integer> future2 = activeObject.calculate(10, 20);
        
        // Do other work while tasks execute
        System.out.println("Doing other work...");
        
        // Get results (blocks if not ready)
        String result1 = future1.get();
        int result2 = future2.get();
        
        System.out.println("Results: " + result1 + ", " + result2);
        
        activeObject.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Active Object Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When you need asynchronous method execution.

2. When you want to simplify concurrency by encapsulating it in objects.

3. When you need to serialize access to an object (methods execute in single thread).

4. When you want to improve responsiveness (caller doesn't block).

5. When you need to schedule method execution (priority, delay).

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **GUI Applications:** Background tasks without freezing UI
- **Game Engines:** AI updates, physics calculations
- **Network Servers:** Handle requests asynchronously
- **Robotics:** Control loops with timing constraints
- **Actors:** Actor model implementations

**Advantages of Active Object Pattern**

1. **Simplified Concurrency:** Each active object has its own thread.

2. **No Explicit Locking:** Methods execute sequentially in one thread.

3. **Responsiveness:** Caller doesn't block.

4. **Scheduling Flexibility:** Can prioritize requests.

5. **Encapsulation:** Concurrency control inside the object.

**Disadvantages of Active Object Pattern**

1. **Overhead:** Creating method request objects for each call.

2. **Complexity:** More complex than direct method calls.

3. **Debugging Difficulty:** Async flow harder to trace.

4. **Resource Usage:** Each active object needs a thread.

5. **Potential for Deadlock:** If active objects call each other.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Active Object | Async method execution | Object with its own thread |
| Future/Promise | Represent async result | Often used with active object |
| Actor Model | Message-passing concurrency | Similar concept |
| Half-Sync/Half-Async | Separate sync/async layers | Different architecture |

**Active Object vs Actor Model**

- **Active Object:** Object-oriented, methods become messages
- **Actor Model:** Pure message-passing, no shared state

**Active Object vs Thread Pool**

- **Active Object:** Each object has dedicated thread
- **Thread Pool:** Threads shared among many tasks

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Too Many Threads:** Each active object needs a thread.

2. **Circular Dependencies:** Can cause deadlock.

3. **Queue Overflow:** Requests may be dropped.

4. **Shutdown Handling:** Need to drain queue properly.

5. **Future Timeouts:** Callers may wait forever.

**Best Practices**

1. **Limit Number of Active Objects:** Each uses a thread.

2. **Use Bounded Queues:** Prevent memory exhaustion.

3. **Implement Timeouts:** Don't wait forever for results.

4. **Handle InterruptedException:** Proper cleanup.

5. **Consider Thread Pool for Many Objects:** Hybrid approach.

6. **Monitor Queue Size:** Detect backpressure.

## Chapter 16: Future / Promise Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Future/Promise pattern represents the result of an asynchronous computation. A Future is a read-only placeholder for a result that will be available later. A Promise is the writable counterpart that completes the Future.

**Understanding the Problem**

When you start an asynchronous operation, you need a way to:

1. Get the result when it's ready
2. Check if the operation is complete
3. Cancel the operation if needed
4. Handle errors that occurred during computation

Without a Future, you'd need callbacks, which can lead to "callback hell" with nested callbacks.

**The Solution**

The Future pattern provides an object that acts as a proxy for a result not yet available. It offers methods to:

- Check if the computation is complete
- Wait for the result (blocking)
- Register callbacks for when result is ready
- Cancel the operation
- Handle exceptions

**Key Concepts**

- **Future:** Read-only view of pending result
- **Promise:** Completable future (can set result)
- **Callback:** Function to execute when result is ready
- **CompletableFuture:** Can chain operations
- **Cancellation:** Ability to cancel pending operation

**Diagram: Future/Promise**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   FUTURE / PROMISE PATTERN                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │               FUTURE LIFECYCLE                         │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   PENDING       │                               │    │
│  │  │ (no result yet) │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                        │    │
│  │           │ set result / exception                 │    │
│  │           │ cancel                                 │    │
│  │           ▼                                        │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐       │    │
│  │  │   COMPLETED     │    │   CANCELLED     │       │    │
│  │  │ (has result)    │    │ (was cancelled) │       │    │
│  │  └─────────────────┘    └─────────────────┘       │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   FAILED        │                               │    │
│  │  │ (has exception) │                               │    │
│  │  └─────────────────┘                               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 FUTURE INTERACTION                     │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐                  │    │
│  │  │ Producer │        │ Consumer │                  │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘        └────┬─────┘                  │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       │ 1. Create Promise │                        │    │
│  │       │───────────────────┼─────────────────┐      │    │
│  │       │                   │                 │      │    │
│  │       │                   │ 2. Return Future│      │    │
│  │       │◀──────────────────┼─────────────────┘      │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       │ 3. Start async    │                        │    │
│  │       │    computation    │                        │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       │                   │ 4. Do other work       │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       │ 5. Complete       │                        │    │
│  │       │    Promise        │                        │    │
│  │       │───result─────────>│                        │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       │                   │ 6. Get result          │    │
│  │       │                   │    (may block if not   │    │
│  │       │                   │     ready)              │    │
│  │       │                   │                        │    │
│  │       ▼                   ▼                        │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              COMPLETABLE FUTURE CHAINING              │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "Hello")       │    │
│  │      .thenApply(s -> s + " World")                  │    │
│  │      .thenApply(String::toUpperCase)                │    │
│  │      .thenAccept(System.out::println)               │    │
│  │      .exceptionally(ex -> {                         │    │
│  │          System.out.println("Error: " + ex);        │    │
│  │          return null;                               │    │
│  │      });                                            │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example (Simple Future)**

```java
public class SimpleFuture<T> {
    private T result;
    private boolean completed = false;
    private Exception error;
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    
    public T get() throws InterruptedException, ExecutionException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            while (!completed) {
                lock.wait();
            }
            if (error != null) {
                throw new ExecutionException(error);
            }
            return result;
        }
    }
    
    public T get(long timeout, TimeUnit unit) 
            throws InterruptedException, ExecutionException, TimeoutException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            long deadline = System.currentTimeMillis() + unit.toMillis(timeout);
            while (!completed) {
                long waitTime = deadline - System.currentTimeMillis();
                if (waitTime <= 0) {
                    throw new TimeoutException();
                }
                lock.wait(waitTime);
            }
            if (error != null) {
                throw new ExecutionException(error);
            }
            return result;
        }
    }
    
    public boolean isDone() {
        synchronized(lock) {
            return completed;
        }
    }
    
    // Promise methods (not in Future interface)
    protected void complete(T result) {
        synchronized(lock) {
            this.result = result;
            this.completed = true;
            lock.notifyAll();
        }
    }
    
    protected void completeExceptionally(Exception error) {
        synchronized(lock) {
            this.error = error;
            this.completed = true;
            lock.notifyAll();
        }
    }
}
```

**Java's CompletableFuture Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class FutureExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        // 1. Simple future with explicit completion
        CompletableFuture<String> future = new CompletableFuture<>();
        
        // In another thread
        new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                Thread.sleep(1000);
                future.complete("Hello from async task");
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                future.completeExceptionally(e);
            }
        }).start();
        
        // Do other work
        System.out.println("Doing other work...");
        
        // Get result (blocks)
        String result = future.get();
        System.out.println("Result: " + result);
        
        // 2. Chained completable futures
        CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
            // First async task
            return "Hello";
        }).thenApply(s -> {
            // Transform result (in same thread or pool)
            return s + " World";
        }).thenApply(String::toUpperCase).thenAccept(s -> {
            // Consume final result
            System.out.println("Final result: " + s);
        }).exceptionally(ex -> {
            // Handle any exception in chain
            System.err.println("Error: " + ex);
            return null;
        });
        
        // 3. Combine multiple futures
        CompletableFuture<String> f1 = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "Hello");
        CompletableFuture<String> f2 = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "World");
        
        CompletableFuture<String> combined = f1.thenCombine(f2, (s1, s2) -> s1 + " " + s2);
        System.out.println("Combined: " + combined.get());
        
        // 4. Wait for any of multiple futures
        CompletableFuture<Object> any = CompletableFuture.anyOf(f1, f2);
        System.out.println("First completed: " + any.get());
        
        // 5. Wait for all futures
        CompletableFuture<Void> all = CompletableFuture.allOf(f1, f2);
        all.get(); // wait for both
        System.out.println("Both completed");
        
        // 6. Async with timeout
        CompletableFuture<String> withTimeout = CompletableFuture
            .supplyAsync(() -> {
                try { Thread.sleep(2000); } catch (Exception e) {}
                return "Done";
            })
            .orTimeout(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
            .exceptionally(ex -> "Timeout occurred");
        
        System.out.println("With timeout: " + withTimeout.get());
    }
}
```

**When to Use Future/Promise Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When performing asynchronous operations.

2. When you need to avoid callback hell.

3. When you want to compose multiple async operations.

4. When you need to handle timeouts and cancellations.

5. When you want to parallelize independent tasks.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Web Clients:** Async HTTP requests
- **Database Queries:** Non-blocking database access
- **File I/O:** Async file operations
- **Microservices:** Parallel service calls
- **UI Applications:** Background tasks without freezing

**Advantages of Future/Promise Pattern**

1. **Abstraction:** Clean representation of async result.

2. **Composability:** Can chain and combine futures.

3. **Non-blocking:** Caller can do other work.

4. **Error Handling:** Unified exception handling.

5. **Cancellation:** Can cancel pending operations.

6. **Timeouts:** Built-in timeout support.

**Disadvantages of Future/Promise Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** More complex than synchronous code.

2. **Memory Leaks:** If futures are never completed.

3. **Debugging Difficulty:** Async stack traces are harder.

4. **Blocking Calls:** get() blocks if not careful.

5. **Thread Usage:** May consume threads.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Future | Async result placeholder | Read-only |
| Promise | Completable future | Can set result |
| Callback | Notification on completion | Inversion of control |
| Reactive Streams | Async stream of data | Multiple values |

**Future vs Callback**

- **Future:** You actively ask for result
- **Callback:** System calls you when ready

**Future vs Reactive Streams**

- **Future:** Single result
- **Reactive Streams:** Stream of results (Observable/Flow)

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Blocking on Future:** Using get() defeats async purpose.

2. **Not Handling Exceptions:** Uncaught exceptions are lost.

3. **Memory Leaks:** Futures never completed.

4. **Thread Starvation:** Too many async tasks.

5. **Callback Hell:** Nested thenApply chains.

**Best Practices**

1. **Prefer Async Methods:** Avoid get() when possible.

2. **Always Handle Exceptions:** Use exceptionally() or handle().

3. **Set Timeouts:** Prevent waiting forever.

4. **Use ExecutorService:** Control thread usage.

5. **Compose, Don't Nest:** Use thenCompose for async operations.

6. **Consider CompletableFuture:** Rich API for composition.

---

[Page 19]

## Chapter 17: Generator Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Generator pattern produces a sequence of values lazily, one at a time, without computing all values at once. Generators maintain their own state between calls and can yield multiple values over time.

**Understanding the Problem**

Sometimes you need to generate a sequence of values, but:

1. The sequence is too large to store in memory (infinite or huge)
2. You want to generate values lazily (on-demand)
3. The generation algorithm is stateful and needs to remember previous state

Examples:
- Fibonacci sequence (infinite)
- Reading a file line by line
- Generating prime numbers
- Paginated API results

**The Solution**

The Generator pattern provides a function that returns an iterator-like object. Each time you ask for the next value, the generator resumes execution from where it left off, maintaining its internal state.

**Key Concepts**

- **Generator Function:** Function that can pause and resume
- **Yield:** Statement that returns a value and pauses execution
- **Iterator:** Object that provides next() method
- **State:** Generator's internal state preserved between calls
- **Lazy Evaluation:** Values computed only when needed

**Diagram: Generator Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      GENERATOR PATTERN                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │               GENERATOR EXECUTION FLOW                 │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Generator Function                                 │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │ function* fibonacci() {                     │   │    │
│  │  │   let a = 0, b = 1;                          │   │    │
│  │  │   while (true) {                             │   │    │
│  │  │     yield a;          // pause and return    │   │    │
│  │  │     [a, b] = [b, a + b]; // resume here      │   │    │
│  │  │   }                                          │   │    │
│  │  │ }                                            │   │    │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Caller                                             │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │ gen = fibonacci()    // create generator     │   │    │
│  │  │ value1 = gen.next()  // runs to first yield  │   │    │
│  │  │ value2 = gen.next()  // resumes, runs to     │   │    │
│  │  │                      // next yield           │   │    │
│  │  │ value3 = gen.next()  // resumes again        │   │    │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              GENERATOR STATE MACHINE                   │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Initial State                                      │    │
│  │       │                                            │    │
│  │       ▼                                            │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   Suspended at  │                               │    │
│  │  │   start         │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │ next() called                          │    │
│  │           ▼                                        │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐                               │    │
│  │  │   Executing     │                               │    │
│  │  │   (running)     │                               │    │
│  │  └────────┬────────┘                               │    │
│  │           │                                        │    │
│  │    ┌──────┴──────┐                                │    │
│  │    │             │                                │    │
│  │    ▼             ▼                                │    │
│  │  yield         return/done                         │    │
│  │    │             │                                │    │
│  │    ▼             ▼                                │    │
│  │  ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐                          │    │
│  │  │Suspended│   │ Done   │                          │    │
│  │  │ at yield│   │        │                          │    │
│  │  └────────┘   └────────┘                          │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              GENERATOR EXAMPLES                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // JavaScript                                      │    │
│  │  function* range(start, end) {                      │    │
│  │    for (let i = start; i <= end; i++) {             │    │
│  │      yield i;                                       │    │
│  │    }                                                │    │
│  │  }                                                  │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Python                                          │    │
│  │  def fibonacci():                                   │    │
│  │      a, b = 0, 1                                    │    │
│  │      while True:                                    │    │
│  │          yield a                                    │    │
│  │          a, b = b, a + b                           │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Java (custom iterator)                          │    │
│  │  Iterator<Integer> fib = new Iterator<>() {         │    │
│  │      int a = 0, b = 1;                              │    │
│  │      public boolean hasNext() { return true; }      │    │
│  │      public Integer next() {                        │    │
│  │          int result = a;                            │    │
│  │          a = b;                                     │    │
│  │          b = result + b;                            │    │
│  │          return result;                             │    │
│  │      }                                              │    │
│  │  };                                                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Examples**

**JavaScript Generator:**

```javascript
// Generator function
function* fibonacci() {
    let a = 0, b = 1;
    while (true) {
        yield a;
        [a, b] = [b, a + b];
    }
}

// Usage
const fib = fibonacci();
console.log(fib.next().value); // 0
console.log(fib.next().value); // 1
console.log(fib.next().value); // 1
console.log(fib.next().value); // 2
console.log(fib.next().value); // 3

// Take first 10 fibonacci numbers
const first10 = Array.from({ length: 10 }, () => fib.next().value);
```

**Python Generator:**

```python
def fibonacci():
    a, b = 0, 1
    while True:
        yield a
        a, b = b, a + b

# Usage
fib = fibonacci()
for i in range(10):
    print(next(fib))

# Generator expression
squares = (x*x for x in range(10))
```

**Java Custom Iterator (simulating generator):**

```java
public class RangeIterator implements Iterator<Integer> {
    private final int end;
    private int current;
    
    public RangeIterator(int start, int end) {
        this.current = start;
        this.end = end;
    }
    
    @Override
    public boolean hasNext() {
        return current <= end;
    }
    
    @Override
    public Integer next() {
        if (!hasNext()) {
            throw new NoSuchElementException();
        }
        return current++; // return and increment state
    }
}

// Usage
Iterator<Integer> range = new RangeIterator(1, 10);
while (range.hasNext()) {
    System.out.println(range.next());
}
```

**Java with Continuations (simulated):**

```java
// Not built-in, but can be simulated with state machine
class FibonacciGenerator {
    private int a = 0;
    private int b = 1;
    private int state = 0;
    
    public Integer next() {
        switch (state) {
            case 0:
                state = 1;
                return 0;
            case 1:
                state = 2;
                return 1;
            default:
                int result = a + b;
                a = b;
                b = result;
                return result;
        }
    }
}
```

**When to Use Generator Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When generating infinite or large sequences.

2. When you want lazy evaluation (compute only when needed).

3. When the generation algorithm is stateful.

4. When processing streams of data.

5. When you want to simplify stateful iteration logic.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Data Streaming:** Reading large files line by line
- **Infinite Sequences:** Fibonacci, prime numbers
- **Paginated APIs:** Fetching next page only when needed
- **Tree Traversal:** In-order, pre-order traversal
- **Event Streams:** Processing events as they arrive

**Advantages of Generator Pattern**

1. **Memory Efficient:** No need to store entire sequence.

2. **Lazy Evaluation:** Compute only what you need.

3. **Infinite Sequences:** Can represent infinite data.

4. **Clean State Management:** State preserved automatically.

5. **Simplifies Code:** Complex iteration logic becomes simple.

**Disadvantages of Generator Pattern**

1. **Language Support:** Not all languages have generators.

2. **One-Time Use:** Generators are typically exhaustible.

3. **Debugging Difficulty:** Execution pauses and resumes.

4. **Overhead:** Some overhead for state preservation.

5. **Not Thread-Safe:** Generators usually single-threaded.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Generator | Lazy sequence generation | Yields multiple values |
| Iterator | Sequential access | Similar, but manual state |
| Observer | Push notifications | Push vs pull |
| Future | Single async result | Single value |

**Generator vs Iterator**

- **Generator:** Automatically maintains state, uses yield
- **Iterator:** Manual state management, next()/hasNext()

**Generator vs Stream (Java 8+)**

- **Generator:** Pull-based, lazy
- **Stream:** Can be lazy or eager, functional operations

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Exhausting Generator:** Can't reuse after completion.

2. **Infinite Loops:** Forgetting to break infinite generators.

3. **State Leakage:** Shared state between generators.

4. **Resource Leaks:** Not closing generator resources.

5. **Thread Safety:** Generators not thread-safe by default.

**Best Practices**

1. **Document Termination:** Clearly indicate if generator is infinite.

2. **Use try-with-resources:** For generators that need cleanup.

3. **Prefer Lazy Evaluation:** Only generate what you need.

4. **Compose Generators:** Combine multiple generators.

5. **Consider Memory:** Generators don't eliminate memory usage entirely.

6. **Test Edge Cases:** Empty sequences, end conditions.

---

[Page 20]

## Chapter 18: Fork-Join Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Fork-Join pattern is a divide-and-conquer approach to parallel processing. A large task is recursively split (forked) into smaller subtasks until they're small enough to solve directly. Results are then combined (joined) to produce the final result.

**Understanding the Problem**

Many problems can be solved by dividing them into smaller, independent subproblems:

- Sorting large arrays (merge sort, quicksort)
- Searching in large datasets
- Matrix multiplication
- Image processing
- Monte Carlo simulations

Doing this sequentially doesn't utilize multiple cores. The challenge is to parallelize the divide-and-conquer algorithm efficiently.

**The Solution**

The Fork-Join pattern provides:

1. **Fork:** Split a task into smaller subtasks that can run in parallel
2. **Join:** Wait for subtasks to complete and combine their results
3. **Work Stealing:** Idle threads steal work from busy threads' queues for better load balancing

**Key Concepts**

- **ForkJoinPool:** Special thread pool for fork-join tasks
- **ForkJoinTask:** Base class for tasks (RecursiveTask for results, RecursiveAction for no result)
- **Work Stealing:** Threads with empty queues steal from others
- **Threshold:** Size at which task is solved directly (not forked)

**Diagram: Fork-Join Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     FORK-JOIN PATTERN                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              TASK DECOMPOSITION                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │                    Problem                           │    │
│  │                        │                             │    │
│  │                        ▼                             │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────┐                     │    │
│  │              │  if too big?    │                     │    │
│  │              └────────┬────────┘                     │    │
│  │                       │                              │    │
│  │              ┌────────┴────────┐                     │    │
│  │              │                 │                     │    │
│  │              ▼                 ▼                     │    │
│  │        ┌─────────┐       ┌─────────┐                 │    │
│  │        │ Fork 1  │       │ Fork 2  │                 │    │
│  │        │ (subtask│       │ (subtask│                 │    │
│  │        │  1)     │       │  2)     │                 │    │
│  │        └────┬────┘       └────┬────┘                 │    │
│  │             │                  │                      │    │
│  │             │                  │                      │    │
│  │        ┌────▼────┐       ┌────▼────┐                 │    │
│  │        │ Compute │       │ Compute │                 │    │
│  │        │  (may   │       │  (may   │                 │    │
│  │        │ fork)   │       │ fork)   │                 │    │
│  │        └────┬────┘       └────┬────┘                 │    │
│  │             │                  │                      │    │
│  │             └────────┬─────────┘                      │    │
│  │                      │                                │    │
│  │                      ▼                                │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────┐                      │    │
│  │              │    Join         │                      │    │
│  │              │ (combine results)│                     │    │
│  │              └────────┬────────┘                      │    │
│  │                       │                               │    │
│  │                       ▼                               │    │
│  │                    Result                             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                WORK STEALING                           │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Thread 1 Queue    Thread 2 Queue    Thread 3 Queue   │    │
│  │  ┌─────────┐      ┌─────────┐      ┌─────────┐      │    │
```
