```
│  │  │ Task A  │      │         │      │ Task D  │      │    │
│  │  │ Task B  │      │ (empty) │      │ Task E  │      │    │
│  │  │ Task C  │      │         │      │ Task F  │      │    │
│  │  └─────────┘      └─────────┘      └─────────┘      │    │
│  │       │                 │                 │          │    │
│  │       └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘          │    │
│  │                         │                            │    │
│  │                         ▼                            │    │
│  │                 Thread 2 steals                      │    │
│  │                 from Thread 1                         │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              FORK-JOIN EXAMPLE                         │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Java Fork-Join for sum of array                  │    │
│  │  class SumTask extends RecursiveTask<Long> {         │    │
│  │      static final int THRESHOLD = 1000;              │    │
│  │      int[] array; int lo, hi;                        │    │
│  │                                                      │    │
│  │      protected Long compute() {                      │    │
│  │          if (hi - lo <= THRESHOLD) {                 │    │
│  │              long sum = 0;                            │    │
│  │              for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++)           │    │
│  │                  sum += array[i];                     │    │
│  │              return sum;                              │    │
│  │          } else {                                     │    │
│  │              int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;                 │    │
│  │              SumTask left = new SumTask(array, lo, mid);│    │
│  │              SumTask right = new SumTask(array, mid, hi);│    │
│  │              left.fork();                             │    │
│  │              long rightResult = right.compute();      │    │
│  │              long leftResult = left.join();           │    │
│  │              return leftResult + rightResult;         │    │
│  │          }                                            │    │
│  │      }                                                │    │
│  │  }                                                   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Usage                                            │    │
│  │  ForkJoinPool pool = new ForkJoinPool();             │    │
│  │  long result = pool.invoke(new SumTask(array, 0, array.length));│
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;

// RecursiveTask for tasks that return a result
class SumTask extends RecursiveTask<Long> {
    private static final int THRESHOLD = 10_000;
    private final int[] array;
    private final int start;
    private final int end;
    
    public SumTask(int[] array, int start, int end) {
        this.array = array;
        this.start = start;
        this.end = end;
    }
    
    @Override
    protected Long compute() {
        int length = end - start;
        
        // If task is small enough, compute directly
        if (length <= THRESHOLD) {
            long sum = 0;
            for (int i = start; i < end; i++) {
                sum += array[i];
            }
            return sum;
        }
        
        // Otherwise, split into two subtasks
        int mid = start + length / 2;
        SumTask leftTask = new SumTask(array, start, mid);
        SumTask rightTask = new SumTask(array, mid, end);
        
        // Fork left task (async)
        leftTask.fork();
        
        // Compute right task directly
        long rightResult = rightTask.compute();
        
        // Wait for left task result
        long leftResult = leftTask.join();
        
        return leftResult + rightResult;
    }
}

// RecursiveAction for tasks that don't return a result
class QuickSortTask extends RecursiveAction {
    private static final int THRESHOLD = 1000;
    private final int[] array;
    private final int left;
    private final int right;
    
    public QuickSortTask(int[] array, int left, int right) {
        this.array = array;
        this.left = left;
        this.right = right;
    }
    
    @Override
    protected void compute() {
        if (left < right) {
            if (right - left < THRESHOLD) {
                // Sequential sort for small arrays
                Arrays.sort(array, left, right + 1);
            } else {
                int pivot = partition(array, left, right);
                
                QuickSortTask leftTask = new QuickSortTask(array, left, pivot - 1);
                QuickSortTask rightTask = new QuickSortTask(array, pivot + 1, right);
                
                // Fork both in parallel
                invokeAll(leftTask, rightTask);
            }
        }
    }
    
    private int partition(int[] array, int left, int right) {
        // Standard quicksort partition
        int pivot = array[right];
        int i = left - 1;
        for (int j = left; j < right; j++) {
            if (array[j] <= pivot) {
                i++;
                swap(array, i, j);
            }
        }
        swap(array, i + 1, right);
        return i + 1;
    }
    
    private void swap(int[] array, int i, int j) {
        int temp = array[i];
        array[i] = array[j];
        array[j] = temp;
    }
}

// Usage
public class ForkJoinExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Create large array
        int[] array = new int[1_000_000];
        Random random = new Random();
        for (int i = 0; i < array.length; i++) {
            array[i] = random.nextInt(100);
        }
        
        // Create fork-join pool (uses number of processors by default)
        ForkJoinPool pool = new ForkJoinPool();
        
        // Compute sum
        SumTask sumTask = new SumTask(array, 0, array.length);
        long sum = pool.invoke(sumTask);
        System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);
        
        // Sort array
        QuickSortTask sortTask = new QuickSortTask(array, 0, array.length - 1);
        pool.invoke(sortTask);
        
        // Check first few elements
        System.out.println("First 10 sorted: " + 
            Arrays.toString(Arrays.copyOf(array, 10)));
        
        // Pool statistics
        System.out.println("Pool size: " + pool.getPoolSize());
        System.out.println("Steal count: " + pool.getStealCount());
        
        pool.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**ForkJoinPool Configuration**

```java
// Create with custom parallelism
ForkJoinPool customPool = new ForkJoinPool(
    4, // parallelism level
    ForkJoinPool.defaultForkJoinWorkerThreadFactory,
    null, // exception handler
    true  // async mode
);

// Common pool (shared across application)
ForkJoinPool commonPool = ForkJoinPool.commonPool();
System.out.println("Common pool parallelism: " + 
    ForkJoinPool.getCommonPoolParallelism());
```

**When to Use Fork-Join Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When problems can be solved with divide-and-conquer.

2. When subtasks are independent (no synchronization needed).

3. When you have multiple cores available.

4. When tasks are CPU-bound (not I/O-bound).

5. When task sizes are irregular (work stealing helps).

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Sorting:** Parallel merge sort, quicksort
- **Searching:** Parallel search in large datasets
- **Matrix Operations:** Multiplication, inversion
- **Image Processing:** Parallel filtering, transformations
- **Data Analysis:** Aggregations, statistics

**Advantages of Fork-Join Pattern**

1. **Automatic Parallelism:** Recursive decomposition maps naturally to parallelism.

2. **Work Stealing:** Excellent load balancing.

3. **Scalability:** Scales with number of cores.

4. **Efficient:** Minimizes overhead of thread creation.

5. **Familiar Model:** Divide-and-conquer is intuitive.

**Disadvantages of Fork-Join Pattern**

1. **Overhead:** Forking and joining has cost.

2. **Threshold Tuning:** Need to choose right threshold.

3. **Not for I/O:** Threads block on I/O, reducing benefits.

4. **Complexity:** More complex than sequential.

5. **Debugging Difficulty:** Parallel execution hard to debug.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Fork-Join | Divide and conquer | Recursive decomposition |
| Thread Pool | General task execution | No built-in work stealing |
| MapReduce | Large-scale data | Map and reduce phases |
| Pipeline | Sequential stages | Linear flow, not tree |

**Fork-Join vs Thread Pool**

- **Fork-Join:** Work stealing, optimized for fork-join tasks
- **Thread Pool:** General purpose, no work stealing

**Fork-Join vs MapReduce**

- **Fork-Join:** In-memory, fine-grained tasks
- **MapReduce:** Distributed, large-scale data processing

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Threshold Too Small:** Too much forking overhead.

2. **Threshold Too Large:** Not enough parallelism.

3. **Blocking Operations:** Threads block, reducing work stealing benefits.

4. **Shared Mutable State:** Requires synchronization.

5. **Not Using join() Properly:** Forgetting to join.

**Best Practices**

1. **Choose Threshold Carefully:** Profile to find optimal size.

2. **Use invokeAll() for Multiple Forks:** More efficient than manual forking.

3. **Avoid Synchronization:** Tasks should be independent.

4. **Use RecursiveTask for Results:** RecursiveAction for side effects.

5. **Consider getSurplusQueuedTaskCount():** For adaptive thresholds.

6. **Monitor Steal Count:** High steal count indicates good load balancing.

7. **Don't Block in Tasks:** Use for CPU-bound work only.

---

[Page 21]

## Chapter 19: Leader-Followers Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Leader-Followers pattern provides an efficient thread pool model where one thread (the leader) waits for events, while other threads (followers) wait their turn to become leader. When an event arrives, the leader handles it and promotes a follower to become the new leader.

**Understanding the Problem**

Traditional thread pool approaches for event handling have drawbacks:

1. **Multiple threads waiting:** All threads block on the same event source, causing contention.

2. **Thread-per-connection:** Creates many threads, not scalable.

3. **Reactor pattern:** Dispatcher thread hands off to worker threads, causing context switches.

**The Solution**

The Leader-Followers pattern maintains a pool of threads where:

- **Leader:** One thread waits for events on the event source
- **Followers:** Other threads wait in a pool to become leader
- When an event arrives, the leader:
  1. Processes the event, OR
  2. Promotes a follower to become the new leader, then processes the event
- Promoted follower becomes the new leader and waits for events

**Key Concepts**

- **Event Source:** Source of events (network socket, queue)
- **Leader:** Thread currently waiting for events
- **Follower Pool:** Threads waiting to become leader
- **Promotion:** Making a follower the new leader
- **Demotion:** Leader becoming follower after processing

**Diagram: Leader-Followers**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   LEADER-FOLLOWERS PATTERN                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │               INITIAL STATE                            │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌─────┐  │    │
│  │  │ Leader   │  │ Follower │  │ Follower │  │ ... │  │    │
│  │  │ (waiting │  │ (waiting │  │ (waiting │  │     │  │    │
│  │  │ on queue)│  │  to be   │  │  to be   │  │     │  │    │
│  │  │          │  │  leader  │  │  leader  │  │     │  │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └─────┘  │    │
│  │       │                                             │    │
│  │       │ Event arrives                               │    │
│  │       ▼                                             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │    │
│  │  │           EVENT PROCESSING                      │  │    │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Option 1: Leader processes event                  │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌─────┐│    │
│  │  │ Leader   │  │ Follower │  │ Follower │  │ ... ││    │
│  │  │(process) │  │ (waiting)│  │ (waiting)│  │     ││    │
│  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └─────┘│    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Option 2: Leader promotes follower                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌─────┐│    │
│  │  │ Old      │  │ New      │  │ Follower │  │ ... ││    │
│  │  │ Leader   │  │ Leader   │  │ (waiting)│  │     ││    │
│  │  │(process) │  │(waiting) │  │          │  │     ││    │
│  │  └──────────┘  └────┬─────┘  └──────────┘  └─────┘│    │
│  │                     │                             │    │
│  │                     │ (promoted)                  │    │
│  │                     ▼                             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 SEQUENCE DIAGRAM                       │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Leader    Follower1   Follower2   Event Source    │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │────wait()─┼───────────┼───────────>│          │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │────wait()─┼───────────>│ (blocked)│    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │           │────wait()─>│ (blocked)│    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │<───event──│           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │──promote─>│           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │────wait()─┼───────────>│          │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │─process─> │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │<───event──│            │          │    │
│  │    │           │──promote─>│           │          │    │
│  │    │           │           │────wait()─>│          │    │
│  │    │           │─process─> │            │          │    │
│  │    │           │           │            │          │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.locks.*;

public class LeaderFollowers {
    private final int threadCount;
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> eventQueue;
    private final Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private final Condition leaderAvailable = lock.newCondition();
    private Thread leader = null;
    private final Set<Thread> followers = ConcurrentHashMap.newKeySet();
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    public LeaderFollowers(int threadCount, BlockingQueue<Runnable> eventQueue) {
        this.threadCount = threadCount;
        this.eventQueue = eventQueue;
        
        // Create all follower threads
        for (int i = 0; i < threadCount; i++) {
            FollowerThread follower = new FollowerThread();
            followers.add(follower);
            follower.start();
        }
        
        // Initially, make one thread the leader
        promoteNewLeader();
    }
    
    private void promoteNewLeader() {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            if (!followers.isEmpty()) {
                // Take a follower and make it leader
                Iterator<Thread> it = followers.iterator();
                leader = it.next();
                followers.remove(leader);
                leaderAvailable.signal(); // Wake up the new leader
            }
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
    
    private void demoteLeader() {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            if (leader != null) {
                followers.add(leader);
                leader = null;
            }
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
    
    private class FollowerThread extends Thread {
        @Override
        public void run() {
            while (running) {
                // Wait to become leader
                lock.lock();
                try {
                    while (leader != this && running) {
                        leaderAvailable.await();
                    }
                    if (!running) break;
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    break;
                } finally {
                    lock.unlock();
                }
                
                // Now we are the leader - wait for event
                try {
                    Runnable event = eventQueue.take();
                    
                    // Before processing, promote a new leader
                    promoteNewLeader();
                    
                    // Process the event
                    event.run();
                    
                    // After processing, we become a follower again
                    demoteLeader();
                    
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    // Interrupted, exit
                    break;
                }
            }
        }
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        lock.lock();
        try {
            leaderAvailable.signalAll();
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
    
    // Example usage
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        BlockingQueue<Runnable> queue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        LeaderFollowers lf = new LeaderFollowers(4, queue);
        
        // Submit some events
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            final int taskId = i;
            queue.add(() -> {
                System.out.println("Processing task " + taskId + 
                    " by " + Thread.currentThread().getName());
                try {
                    Thread.sleep(100); // Simulate work
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                }
            });
        }
        
        // Let it run for a while
        try {
            Thread.sleep(5000);
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
        }
        
        lf.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**Simpler Implementation Using Executor**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class SimpleLeaderFollowers {
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> eventQueue;
    private final ExecutorService workerPool;
    private volatile Thread leader;
    
    public SimpleLeaderFollowers(int threadCount, BlockingQueue<Runnable> queue) {
        this.eventQueue = queue;
        this.workerPool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadCount - 1);
        
        // Main thread becomes leader
        this.leader = Thread.currentThread();
        
        // Start follower threads
        for (int i = 0; i < threadCount - 1; i++) {
            workerPool.submit(this::followerTask);
        }
    }
    
    public void start() {
        while (!Thread.interrupted()) {
            try {
                // Leader waits for event
                Runnable event = eventQueue.take();
                
                // Promote follower (submit to pool)
                workerPool.submit(() -> {
                    // This follower will become temporary leader
                    // In real implementation, more sophisticated
                    event.run();
                });
                
                // Leader continues as leader (in this simple version)
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                break;
            }
        }
    }
    
    private void followerTask() {
        // Followers just wait to be used
        try {
            Thread.sleep(Long.MAX_VALUE);
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            // Expected when promoted
        }
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        workerPool.shutdownNow();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Leader-Followers Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When handling many events from a single source.

2. When you want to minimize context switching.

3. In high-performance network servers.

4. When event processing is relatively quick.

5. When you need predictable latency.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Network Servers:** Handling client connections
- **Event Processing Systems:** Processing events from a queue
- **Real-time Systems:** Where latency is critical
- **Database Listeners:** Waiting for notifications
- **Message Queue Consumers:** Processing messages

**Advantages of Leader-Followers Pattern**

1. **Reduced Context Switching:** Only one thread waits for events.

2. **Cache Efficiency:** Leader's cache warm for event handling.

3. **No Handoff:** Event handled directly by leader (option 1).

4. **Scalability:** Can add more followers.

5. **Simplicity:** Clear leader/follower roles.

**Disadvantages of Leader-Followers Pattern**

1. **Complex Implementation:** Promotion/demotion logic.

2. **Single Point of Contention:** Leader promotion requires synchronization.

3. **Not Suitable for Slow Processing:** If event processing is slow, other events wait.

4. **Priority Inversion:** Low-priority event might be handled first.

5. **Debugging Difficulty:** Dynamic roles hard to trace.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Leader-Followers | Event handling with one waiter | Single leader waits |
| Reactor | Demultiplex events | Dispatcher thread |
| Thread Pool | General task execution | All threads wait |
| Half-Sync/Half-Async | Separate sync/async layers | Different architecture |

**Leader-Followers vs Reactor**

- **Leader-Followers:** Leader thread waits, promotes followers
- **Reactor:** Dispatcher thread waits, hands off to workers

**Leader-Followers vs Half-Sync/Half-Async**

- **Leader-Followers:** All threads in same layer
- **Half-Sync/Half-Async:** Separates sync and async layers

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Race Conditions:** In promotion logic.

2. **Thread Starvation:** If promotion is slow.

3. **Deadlock:** If leader waits for follower that's waiting for leader.

4. **Priority Issues:** No guarantee of event priority.

5. **Complex Shutdown:** Need to cleanly terminate all threads.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Non-blocking Promotion:** Avoid holding locks during promotion.

2. **Consider Timeout:** Leader should timeout if no events.

3. **Monitor Queue Size:** Detect backpressure.

4. **Implement Graceful Shutdown:** Allow in-progress events to complete.

5. **Profile Contention:** Measure leader promotion overhead.

6. **Consider Hybrid Approaches:** Combine with thread pool for slow operations.

---

[Page 22]

# PART FOUR: DATA FLOW PATTERNS

## Chapter 20: Producer-Consumer Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Producer-Consumer pattern separates the production of data from its consumption using a shared queue. Producers generate data and put it into the queue; consumers take data from the queue and process it.

**Understanding the Problem**

In many systems, data production and consumption happen at different rates:

- Producers may generate data faster than consumers can process
- Consumers may be idle when no data is available
- Multiple producers and consumers need to coordinate

Without proper coordination:
- Producers might overload consumers
- Consumers might waste CPU polling for data
- Shared data structures might be corrupted

**The Solution**

The Producer-Consumer pattern uses a thread-safe queue as a buffer between producers and consumers:

- **Producers:** Add items to the queue (block if queue is full)
- **Consumers:** Remove items from the queue (block if queue is empty)
- **Queue:** Thread-safe, bounded or unbounded

**Key Concepts**

- **Producer:** Thread(s) that create data
- **Consumer:** Thread(s) that process data
- **Queue:** Buffer between producers and consumers
- **Bounded Queue:** Limits number of items (provides backpressure)
- **Blocking:** Threads wait when queue is full/empty

**Diagram: Producer-Consumer**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   PRODUCER-CONSUMER PATTERN                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                     PRODUCERS                          │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │    │
│  │  │Producer 1│  │Producer 2│  │Producer 3│          │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘          │    │
│  │       │             │             │                 │    │
│  │       │ produce     │ produce     │ produce         │    │
│  │       ▼             ▼             ▼                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                  SHARED QUEUE                          │    │
│  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐    │    │
│  │  │Item1│ │Item2│ │Item3│ │Item4│ │Item5│ │ ... │    │    │
│  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘    │    │
│  │  <───── enqueue ──────        ────── dequeue ─────> │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                        │                                    │
│                        ▼                                    │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                     CONSUMERS                          │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │    │
│  │  │Consumer 1│  │Consumer 2│  │Consumer 3│          │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘          │    │
│  │       │             │             │                 │    │
│  │       │ consume     │ consume     │ consume         │    │
│  │       ▼             ▼             ▼                 │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              BLOCKING BEHAVIOR                         │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  When queue is empty:                               │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐             │    │
│  │  │Consumer  │─────▶│  Block until    │             │    │
│  │  │ wants    │      │  item available │             │    │
│  │  │ to take  │      └─────────────────┘             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  When queue is full:                               │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐             │    │
│  │  │Producer  │─────▶│  Block until    │             │    │
│  │  │ wants    │      │  space available │             │    │
│  │  │ to put   │      └─────────────────┘             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.*;

// 1. Simple implementation with wait/notify
class SimpleBoundedBuffer<T> {
    private final T[] buffer;
    private int count = 0;
    private int putIndex = 0;
    private int takeIndex = 0;
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    
    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    public SimpleBoundedBuffer(int capacity) {
        buffer = (T[]) new Object[capacity];
    }
    
    public void put(T item) throws InterruptedException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            while (count == buffer.length) {
                lock.wait(); // buffer full, wait
            }
            
            buffer[putIndex] = item;
            putIndex = (putIndex + 1) % buffer.length;
            count++;
            
            lock.notifyAll(); // notify waiting consumers
        }
    }
    
    public T take() throws InterruptedException {
        synchronized(lock) {
            while (count == 0) {
                lock.wait(); // buffer empty, wait
            }
            
            T item = buffer[takeIndex];
            takeIndex = (takeIndex + 1) % buffer.length;
            count--;
            
            lock.notifyAll(); // notify waiting producers
            
            return item;
        }
    }
}

// 2. Using Java's BlockingQueue
class ProducerConsumerExample {
    private static final int CAPACITY = 10;
    private static final int PRODUCER_COUNT = 2;
    private static final int CONSUMER_COUNT = 3;
    
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        BlockingQueue<Integer> queue = new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(CAPACITY);
        
        // Start producers
        for (int i = 0; i < PRODUCER_COUNT; i++) {
            new Thread(new Producer(queue, "P" + i)).start();
        }
        
        // Start consumers
        for (int i = 0; i < CONSUMER_COUNT; i++) {
            new Thread(new Consumer(queue, "C" + i)).start();
        }
    }
    
    static class Producer implements Runnable {
        private final BlockingQueue<Integer> queue;
        private final String name;
        private static int counter = 0;
        
        Producer(BlockingQueue<Integer> queue, String name) {
            this.queue = queue;
            this.name = name;
        }
        
        public void run() {
            try {
                while (true) {
                    int item = counter++;
                    queue.put(item); // blocks if queue full
                    System.out.println(name + " produced: " + item);
                    Thread.sleep(100); // simulate work
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        }
    }
    
    static class Consumer implements Runnable {
        private final BlockingQueue<Integer> queue;
        private final String name;
        
        Consumer(BlockingQueue<Integer> queue, String name) {
            this.queue = queue;
            this.name = name;
        }
        
        public void run() {
            try {
                while (true) {
                    Integer item = queue.take(); // blocks if queue empty
                    System.out.println(name + " consumed: " + item);
                    Thread.sleep(200); // simulate work (slower than producer)
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        }
    }
}

// 3. Multiple producers/consumers with poison pill
class PoisonPillExample {
    private static final int POISON_PILL = -1;
    
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        BlockingQueue<Integer> queue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        
        // Start producers
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            new Thread(() -> {
                for (int j = 0; j < 10; j++) {
                    try {
                        queue.put(j);
                    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                        Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                    }
                }
                // Signal completion
                try {
                    queue.put(POISON_PILL);
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                }
            }).start();
        }
        
        // Consumer that stops on poison pill
        new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                int poisonCount = 0;
                while (poisonCount < 3) { // wait for all producers
                    int item = queue.take();
                    if (item == POISON_PILL) {
                        poisonCount++;
                    } else {
                        System.out.println("Processing: " + item);
                    }
                }
                System.out.println("All done");
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        }).start();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Producer-Consumer Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When data production and consumption rates differ.

2. When you have multiple producers and/or consumers.

3. When you want to decouple production from processing.

4. When you need to handle bursty workloads.

5. In pipeline and workflow systems.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Logging Systems:** Application produces logs, consumers write to disk
- **Message Queues:** RabbitMQ, Kafka, ActiveMQ
- **Web Servers:** Request queue between accept and worker threads
- **Image Processing:** Upload images, process asynchronously
- **Print Spoolers:** Applications produce print jobs, printer consumes

**Advantages of Producer-Consumer Pattern**

1. **Decoupling:** Producers and consumers don't know each other.

2. **Load Leveling:** Queue smooths out bursty production.

3. **Flexible Scaling:** Can add more producers/consumers independently.

4. **Backpressure:** Bounded queue prevents overload.

5. **Thread Safety:** Queue handles synchronization.

**Disadvantages of Producer-Consumer Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** More complex than direct calls.

2. **Queue Overhead:** Memory and synchronization overhead.

3. **Potential for Stale Data:** Items may wait in queue.

4. **Deadlock Risk:** If producers and consumers block each other.

5. **Queue Size Dilemma:** Too small causes blocking, too large uses memory.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Producer-Consumer | Buffer between prod/cons | Core pattern |
| Pipeline | Sequential stages | Multiple queues in series |
| Publish-Subscribe | Multiple subscribers | Broadcast, not point-to-point |
| Active Object | Async method execution | Each object has queue |

**Producer-Consumer vs Pipeline**

- **Producer-Consumer:** Single queue between producers and consumers
- **Pipeline:** Multiple stages, each with its own queue

**Producer-Consumer vs Publish-Subscribe**

- **Producer-Consumer:** Each item consumed by one consumer
- **Publish-Subscribe:** Each item goes to all subscribers

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Unbounded Queues:** Can cause memory exhaustion.

2. **Lost Wakeups:** Signaling before consumer waits.

3. **Poison Pill Issues:** Not handling multiple producers.

4. **Thread Leakage:** Not handling interrupted exceptions.

5. **Priority Inversion:** Low-priority items processed first.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Bounded Queues:** Prevent memory exhaustion.

2. **Implement Backpressure:** Slow producers when queue is full.

3. **Use Poison Pills:** For graceful shutdown.

4. **Monitor Queue Size:** Alert on queue buildup.

5. **Consider Priorities:** Use PriorityQueue if needed.

6. **Handle InterruptedException:** Proper cleanup.

7. **Test Under Load:** Verify behavior at limits.

---

[Page 23]

## Chapter 21: Pipeline Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Pipeline pattern chains together multiple processing stages, where each stage performs a specific operation on data and passes the result to the next stage. Data flows through the pipeline like an assembly line.

**Understanding the Problem**

Complex data processing often requires multiple steps:

- Read data → Parse → Validate → Transform → Enrich → Store
- Image: Load → Resize → Filter → Compress → Save
- Video: Decode → Process frames → Encode

Doing all steps sequentially uses only one thread. But different steps can often be parallelized if we pipeline them.

**The Solution**

The Pipeline pattern breaks processing into stages connected by queues. Each stage runs in its own thread (or thread pool), processing items from its input queue and passing results to the next stage's queue.

**Key Concepts**

- **Stage:** Processing unit with input and output queues
- **Pipeline:** Sequence of stages
- **Data Item:** Unit of work flowing through pipeline
- **Parallelism:** Multiple items can be in different stages simultaneously
- **Backpressure:** Slow stages cause upstream queues to fill

**Diagram: Pipeline Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                       PIPELINE PATTERN                        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                   PIPELINE STAGES                      │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Input ──▶ Stage 1 ──▶ Stage 2 ──▶ Stage 3 ──▶ Output  │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │  │  Queue   │   │  Queue   │   │  Queue   │        │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────┐ │   │  ┌─────┐ │   │  ┌─────┐ │        │    │
│  │  │  │Item3│ │   │  │Item4│ │   │  │Item5│ │        │    │
│  │  │  ├─────┤ │   │  ├─────┤ │   │  ├─────┤ │        │    │
│  │  │  │Item2│ │   │  │Item3│ │   │  │Item4│ │        │    │
│  │  │  ├─────┤ │   │  ├─────┤ │   │  ├─────┤ │        │    │
│  │  │  │Item1│ │   │  │Item2│ │   │  │Item3│ │        │    │
│  │  │  └─────┘ │   │  └─────┘ │   │  └─────┘ │        │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘        │    │
│  │       │              │              │               │    │
│  │       │ take         │ take         │ take          │    │
│  │       ▼              ▼              ▼               │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │  │ Stage 1  │──▶│ Stage 2  │──▶│ Stage 3  │──▶ ... │    │
│  │  │ Thread   │   │ Thread   │   │ Thread   │        │    │
│  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘        │    │
│  │       │              │              │               │    │
│  │       │ put          │ put          │ put           │    │
│  │       ▼              ▼              ▼               │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │  │ Queue    │   │ Queue    │   │ Output   │        │    │
│  │  │ to Stage2│   │ to Stage3│   │          │        │    │
│  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘        │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                  PIPELINE EXAMPLE                      │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Image processing pipeline                        │    │
│  │  Source ──▶ Decode ──▶ Resize ──▶ Filter ──▶ Encode ──▶ Save │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Time 0: Image1 at Decode                          │    │
│  │  Time 1: Image1 at Resize, Image2 at Decode        │    │
│  │  Time 2: Image1 at Filter, Image2 at Resize, Image3 at Decode│
│  │  Time 3: Image1 at Encode, Image2 at Filter, ...   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Throughput = 1 image per time unit (once pipeline full)│
│  │  Latency = 4 time units (from start to finish)     │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.function.Function;

// 1. Generic Pipeline Stage
class PipelineStage<I, O> {
    private final BlockingQueue<I> inputQueue;
    private final BlockingQueue<O> outputQueue;
    private final Function<I, O> processor;
    private final Thread thread;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    public PipelineStage(Function<I, O> processor, int queueCapacity) {
        this.inputQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueCapacity);
        this.outputQueue = null; // for last stage
        this.processor = processor;
        this.thread = new Thread(this::run);
    }
    
    public PipelineStage(Function<I, O> processor, 
                         BlockingQueue<O> outputQueue,
                         int queueCapacity) {
        this.inputQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueCapacity);
        this.outputQueue = outputQueue;
        this.processor = processor;
        this.thread = new Thread(this::run);
    }
    
    public void start() {
        thread.start();
    }
    
    public void stop() {
        running = false;
        thread.interrupt();
    }
    
    public void submit(I item) throws InterruptedException {
        inputQueue.put(item);
    }
    
    private void run() {
        while (running) {
            try {
                I item = inputQueue.take();
                O result = processor.apply(item);
                
                if (outputQueue != null) {
                    outputQueue.put(result);
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                break;
            }
        }
    }
}

// 2. Pipeline Builder
class PipelineBuilder<T> {
    private final BlockingQueue<T> sourceQueue;
    private PipelineStage<?, ?> lastStage;
    
    public PipelineBuilder(BlockingQueue<T> sourceQueue) {
        this.sourceQueue = sourceQueue;
    }
    
    public <R> PipelineBuilder<R> addStage(
            Function<T, R> processor, int queueCapacity) {
        
        BlockingQueue<R> queue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueCapacity);
        PipelineStage<T, R> stage = new PipelineStage<>(
            processor, queue, queueCapacity);
        
        // Connect previous stage to this one
        if (lastStage == null) {
            // First stage - connect to source
            new Thread(() -> {
                try {
                    while (true) {
                        T item = sourceQueue.take();
                        stage.submit(item);
                    }
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                }
            }).start();
        }
        
        stage.start();
        lastStage = stage;
        
        return (PipelineBuilder<R>) this;
    }
    
    public void submit(T item) throws InterruptedException {
        sourceQueue.put(item);
    }
}

// 3. Complete Pipeline Example
public class PipelineExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        // Create pipeline stages
        BlockingQueue<String> input = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        BlockingQueue<String> output = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        
        // Stage 1: Read and clean
        PipelineStage<String, String> stage1 = new PipelineStage<>(
            s -> {
                System.out.println("Stage1 cleaning: " + s);
                return s.trim().toLowerCase();
            },
            new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(10),
            10
        );
        
        // Stage 2: Parse
        PipelineStage<String, String[]> stage2 = new PipelineStage<>(
            s -> {
                System.out.println("Stage2 parsing: " + s);
                return s.split("\\s+");
            },
            new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(10),
            10
        );
        
        // Stage 3: Count words
        PipelineStage<String[], Integer> stage3 = new PipelineStage<>(
            words -> {
                System.out.println("Stage3 counting: " + Arrays.toString(words));
                return words.length;
            },
            output,
            10
        );
        
        // Connect stages
        // input -> stage1 -> stage2 -> stage3 -> output
        
        // Connect input to stage1
        new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                while (true) {
                    String item = input.take();
                    stage1.submit(item);
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        }).start();
        
        // Connect stage1 to stage2
        // (In a real implementation, stages would be connected directly)
        new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                while (true) {
                    // This is simplified; real implementation would have
                    // stage1's output queue connected to stage2's input
                }
            } catch (Exception e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        }).start();
        
        // Start stages
        stage1.start();
        stage2.start();
        stage3.start();
        
        // Submit work
        input.put("Hello World");
        input.put("This is a test");
        input.put("Pipeline pattern example");
        
        // Collect results
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            Integer result = output.take();
            System.out.println("Word count: " + result);
        }
        
        // Cleanup
        stage1.stop();
        stage2.stop();
        stage3.stop();
    }
}
```

**Better Implementation Using ExecutorService**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.function.Function;

class PipelineStage2<I, O> {
    private final BlockingQueue<I> inputQueue;
    private final BlockingQueue<O> outputQueue;
    private final Function<I, O> processor;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private final String name;
    
    public PipelineStage2(String name, Function<I, O> processor, 
                          int queueCapacity, ExecutorService executor) {
        this.name = name;
        this.inputQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueCapacity);
        this.outputQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        this.processor = processor;
        this.executor = executor;
        
        start();
    }
    
    private void start() {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                while (!Thread.interrupted()) {
                    I item = inputQueue.take();
                    O result = processor.apply(item);
                    outputQueue.put(result);
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        });
    }
    
    public void submit(I item) throws InterruptedException {
        inputQueue.put(item);
    }
    
    public O take() throws InterruptedException {
        return outputQueue.take();
    }
    
    public BlockingQueue<O> getOutputQueue() {
        return outputQueue;
    }
}

class PipelineBuilder2 {
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private BlockingQueue<?> lastOutputQueue;
    
    public PipelineBuilder2(ExecutorService executor) {
        this.executor = executor;
    }
    
    public <I, O> PipelineBuilder2 addStage(String name, 
                                            Function<I, O> processor,
                                            int queueCapacity) {
        PipelineStage2<I, O> stage = new PipelineStage2<>(
            name, processor, queueCapacity, executor);
        
        if (lastOutputQueue != null) {
            // Connect previous stage to this one
            BlockingQueue<I> prevQueue = (BlockingQueue<I>) lastOutputQueue;
            connectQueues(prevQueue, stage);
        }
        
        lastOutputQueue = stage.getOutputQueue();
        return this;
    }
    
    private <I> void connectQueues(BlockingQueue<I> source, 
                                   PipelineStage2<I, ?> target) {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                while (!Thread.interrupted()) {
                    I item = source.take();
                    target.submit(item);
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        });
    }
    
    public BlockingQueue<?> getOutputQueue() {
        return lastOutputQueue;
    }
}
```

**When to Use Pipeline Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When processing has multiple distinct stages.

2. When stages can operate in parallel.

3. When you want to increase throughput through parallelism.

4. When each stage can be developed independently.

5. When you need to process streams of data.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **ETL Pipelines:** Extract, Transform, Load
- **Video Processing:** Decode, process, encode
- **Compiler Design:** Lexical analysis, parsing, code generation
- **Image Processing:** Load, resize, filter, save
- **Data Analysis Pipelines:** Filter, map, reduce

**Advantages of Pipeline Pattern**

1. **Parallelism:** Multiple items processed simultaneously.

2. **Modularity:** Stages are independent and reusable.

3. **Scalability:** Can scale individual stages.

4. **Throughput:** Increases overall throughput.

5. **Decoupling:** Stages don't need to know about each other.

**Disadvantages of Pipeline Pattern**

1. **Latency:** First item takes time to go through pipeline.

2. **Complexity:** More complex than sequential processing.

3. **Load Balancing:** Fast stages may wait for slow ones.

4. **Backpressure:** Slow stages cause upstream queues to fill.

5. **Debugging Difficulty:** Hard to trace items through pipeline.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Pipeline | Sequential stages | Linear flow |
| Producer-Consumer | Single queue | Building block for pipeline |
| Fork-Join | Tree decomposition | Not linear |
| MapReduce | Map then reduce | Two stages only |

**Pipeline vs Producer-Consumer**

- **Pipeline:** Multiple stages in series
- **Producer-Consumer:** Single stage with queue

**Pipeline vs Fork-Join**

- **Pipeline:** Linear flow, items flow through
- **Fork-Join:** Tree decomposition, combine results

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Unbounded Queues:** Memory exhaustion.

2. **Uneven Stage Speeds:** Fast stages blocked by slow ones.

3. **Deadlocks:** Circular dependencies between stages.

4. **Thread Leaks:** Not shutting down stages properly.

5. **Error Handling:** Errors in one stage affect others.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Bounded Queues:** Provide backpressure.

2. **Monitor Queue Sizes:** Detect bottlenecks.

3. **Implement Timeouts:** Prevent indefinite blocking.

4. **Handle Errors Gracefully:** Poison pills or error queues.

5. **Balance Stage Speeds:** Consider parallelizing slow stages.

6. **Test with Various Loads:** Verify pipeline behavior.

7. **Consider Circuit Breakers:** Prevent cascade failures.

---

[Page 24]

## Chapter 22: Fan-Out / Fan-In Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Fan-Out/Fan-In pattern distributes work to multiple concurrent workers (fan-out) and then combines their results (fan-in). It's useful for parallelizing independent subtasks.

**Understanding the Problem**

Many tasks can be broken into independent subtasks:

- Processing multiple files simultaneously
- Querying multiple databases or services
- Performing independent calculations
- Fetching multiple web pages

Doing these sequentially is slow. But if we do them in parallel, we need to wait for all results and combine them.

**The Solution**

The Fan-Out/Fan-In pattern provides:

1. **Fan-Out:** Distribute work to multiple workers (threads, processes, services)
2. **Parallel Processing:** Workers execute independently
3. **Fan-In:** Collect and combine results from all workers
4. **Synchronization:** Wait for all workers to complete

**Key Concepts**

- **Splitter:** Distributes work items to workers
- **Workers:** Process individual work items
- **Joiner:** Collects and combines results
- **Aggregator:** Combines partial results
- **Barrier:** Waits for all workers to complete

**Diagram: Fan-Out / Fan-In**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   FAN-OUT / FAN-IN PATTERN                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                      FAN-OUT                           │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │                    ┌──────────┐                     │    │
│  │                    │  Input   │                     │    │
│  │                    │  Task    │                     │    │
│  │                    └────┬─────┘                     │    │
│  │                         │                           │    │
│  │                         │ split                      │    │
│  │                         ▼                           │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────────┐                │    │
│  │              │     Splitter        │                │    │
│  │              └──────────┬──────────┘                │    │
│  │               ┌──────────┼──────────┐               │    │
│  │               │          │          │               │    │
│  │               ▼          ▼          ▼               │    │
│  │        ┌──────────┐┌──────────┐┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │        │ Worker 1 ││ Worker 2 ││ Worker 3 │        │    │
│  │        │ (task1)  ││ (task2)  ││ (task3)  │        │    │
│  │        └────┬─────┘└────┬─────┘└────┬─────┘        │    │
│  │             │           │           │               │    │
│  │             └───────────┼───────────┘               │    │
│  │                         │                           │    │
│  │                         ▼                           │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────────┐                │    │
│  │              │      Joiner         │                │    │
│  │              └──────────┬──────────┘                │    │
│  │                         │                           │    │
│  │                         │ combine                    │    │
│  │                         ▼                           │    │
│  │                    ┌──────────┐                     │    │
│  │                    │  Result  │                     │    │
│  │                    └──────────┘                     │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 FAN-OUT / FAN-IN EXAMPLES              │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // 1. Parallel file processing                     │    │
│  │  List<File> files = getFiles();                     │    │
│  │  List<CompletableFuture<Result>> futures =          │    │
│  │      files.stream()                                 │    │
│  │          .map(f -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(   │    │
│  │              () -> processFile(f), executor))       │    │
│  │          .collect(Collectors.toList());             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Fan-in                                          │    │
│  │  CompletableFuture.allOf(futures.toArray(new       │    │
│  │      CompletableFuture[0])).join();                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  List<Result> results = futures.stream()           │    │
│  │      .map(CompletableFuture::join)                  │    │
│  │      .collect(Collectors.toList());                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // 2. Parallel service calls                       │    │
│  │  CompletableFuture<User> user =                      │    │
│  │      CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> getUser(id));│    │
│  │  CompletableFuture<Orders> orders =                  │    │
│  │      CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> getOrders(id));│
│  │  CompletableFuture<Reviews> reviews =                │    │
│  │      CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> getReviews(id));│
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  CompletableFuture.allOf(user, orders, reviews).join();│
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.stream.*;

// 1. Simple Fan-Out/Fan-In with CompletableFuture
class FanOutFanInExample {
    private final ExecutorService executor = 
        Executors.newFixedThreadPool(10);
    
    public List<Result> processFiles(List<File> files) {
        // Fan-out: submit all tasks
        List<CompletableFuture<Result>> futures = files.stream()
            .map(file -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> processFile(file), executor))
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
        
        // Fan-in: wait for all
        CompletableFuture<Void> allDone = CompletableFuture
            .allOf(futures.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0]));
        
        // This blocks until all complete
        allDone.join();
        
        // Collect results
        return futures.stream()
            .map(CompletableFuture::join)
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
    }
    
    private Result processFile(File file) {
        // Simulate processing
        return new Result(file.getName(), file.length());
    }
}

// 2. Custom Fan-Out/Fan-In Implementation
class FanOutFanIn<T, R> {
    private final int workerCount;
    private final Function<T, R> worker;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    
    public FanOutFanIn(int workerCount, Function<T, R> worker) {
        this.workerCount = workerCount;
        this.worker = worker;
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(workerCount);
    }
    
    public List<R> process(List<T> items) throws InterruptedException {
        // Create a queue for results
        BlockingQueue<R> results = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        
        // Fan-out: submit all tasks
        CountDownLatch latch = new CountDownLatch(items.size());
        
        for (T item : items) {
            executor.submit(() -> {
                try {
                    R result = worker.apply(item);
                    results.put(result);
                } catch (Exception e) {
                    // Handle error
                } finally {
                    latch.countDown();
                }
            });
        }
        
        // Fan-in: wait for all to complete
        latch.await();
        
        // Collect all results
        List<R> resultList = new ArrayList<>();
        results.drainTo(resultList);
        return resultList;
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 3. Parallel Service Aggregator
class ServiceAggregator {
    private final ExecutorService executor = 
        Executors.newFixedThreadPool(3);
    
    public UserProfile getUserProfile(String userId) {
        // Fan-out: call services in parallel
        CompletableFuture<UserInfo> userInfoFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> getUserInfo(userId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<List<Order>> ordersFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> getOrders(userId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<List<Review>> reviewsFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> getReviews(userId), executor);
        
        // Fan-in: wait for all and combine
        return CompletableFuture
            .allOf(userInfoFuture, ordersFuture, reviewsFuture)
            .thenApply(v -> {
                UserInfo info = userInfoFuture.join();
                List<Order> orders = ordersFuture.join();
                List<Review> reviews = reviewsFuture.join();
                return new UserProfile(info, orders, reviews);
            })
            .join(); // Block for demo
    }
    
    private UserInfo getUserInfo(String userId) {
        // Simulate API call
        return new UserInfo(userId, "John Doe");
    }
    
    private List<Order> getOrders(String userId) {
        // Simulate database query
        return Arrays.asList(new Order("1", 100), new Order("2", 200));
    }
    
    private List<Review> getReviews(String userId) {
        // Simulate API call
        return Arrays.asList(new Review("Great!", 5));
    }
}

// 4. Scatter-Gather Implementation
class ScatterGather<T, R> {
    private final List<Function<T, R>> workers;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    
    public ScatterGather(List<Function<T, R>> workers, int threadPoolSize) {
        this.workers = workers;
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadPoolSize);
    }
    
    public List<R> gather(T input, long timeout, TimeUnit unit) 
            throws InterruptedException, TimeoutException {
        
        List<CompletableFuture<R>> futures = workers.stream()
            .map(worker -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> worker.apply(input), executor))
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
        
        CompletableFuture<Void> all = CompletableFuture
            .allOf(futures.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0]));
        
        try {
            all.get(timeout, unit);
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            // Handle
        }
        
        // Return completed results (some may be incomplete on timeout)
        return futures.stream()
            .filter(f -> f.isDone() && !f.isCompletedExceptionally())
            .map(CompletableFuture::join)
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
    }
}
```

**When to Use Fan-Out / Fan-In Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When tasks can be broken into independent subtasks.

2. When you need to call multiple services in parallel.

3. When processing multiple files or data items.

4. When you need to aggregate results from multiple sources.

5. In map-reduce style computations.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Web Scraping:** Fetch multiple pages in parallel
- **Microservices:** Parallel service calls
- **Data Processing:** Process multiple files
- **Search Engines:** Query multiple shards
- **Batch Processing:** Parallel job execution

**Advantages of Fan-Out / Fan-In Pattern**

1. **Performance:** Significant speedup for independent tasks.

2. **Scalability:** Can add more workers.

3. **Resilience:** Failure of one worker doesn't affect others.

4. **Simplicity:** Clear pattern for parallel processing.

5. **Resource Efficiency:** Can limit concurrent workers.

**Disadvantages of Fan-Out / Fan-In Pattern**

1. **Resource Usage:** Many threads consume resources.

2. **Overhead:** Creating many tasks has overhead.

3. **Error Handling:** Complex to handle partial failures.

4. **Load Imbalance:** Some workers may finish early.

5. **Result Aggregation:** Combining results can be complex.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Fan-Out/Fan-In | Parallel independent tasks | Split and combine |
| Fork-Join | Recursive decomposition | Tree structure |
| MapReduce | Map then reduce | Two-phase, distributed |
| Pipeline | Sequential stages | Linear flow |

**Fan-Out/Fan-In vs Fork-Join**

- **Fan-Out/Fan-In:** Independent tasks, no dependencies
- **Fork-Join:** Recursive, tasks may fork further

**Fan-Out/Fan-In vs MapReduce**

- **Fan-Out/Fan-In:** General parallel pattern
- **MapReduce:** Specialized for large datasets with map and reduce

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Too Many Threads:** Overwhelming the system.

2. **Not Handling Errors:** One failure shouldn't stop all.

3. **Memory Leaks:** Futures not cleaned up.

4. **Deadlocks:** If tasks depend on each other.

5. **Timeout Issues:** Not setting timeouts for external calls.

**Best Practices**

1. **Limit Concurrency:** Use bounded thread pools.

2. **Handle Partial Results:** Decide what to do if some tasks fail.

3. **Set Timeouts:** Prevent waiting forever.

4. **Use CompletableFuture:** For clean composition.

5. **Monitor Queue Sizes:** Detect bottlenecks.

6. **Consider Cancellation:** Cancel remaining tasks if one fails.

7. **Test with Various Loads:** Verify behavior at limits.

---

[Page 25]

## Chapter 23: MapReduce Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The MapReduce pattern processes large datasets by dividing work into map and reduce phases. It's designed for distributed processing across many machines.

**Understanding the Problem**

Processing extremely large datasets (terabytes or petabytes) on a single machine is impossible due to:

1. **Storage Limits:** Data too large for one machine
2. **Processing Limits:** Would take too long
3. **Fault Tolerance:** Single machine failure stops everything

**The Solution**

MapReduce divides processing into two phases:

1. **Map Phase:** 
   - Split input into chunks
   - Process chunks in parallel across many machines
   - Each map task produces key-value pairs

2. **Shuffle Phase:**
   - Sort and group by key
   - Transfer data from mappers to reducers

3. **Reduce Phase:**
   - Process each group of values for a key
   - Produce final results

**Key Concepts**

- **Mapper:** Processes input chunk, emits key-value pairs
- **Reducer:** Aggregates values for each key
- **Shuffle:** Sorts and groups by key between phases
- **Partitioner:** Distributes keys to reducers
- **Combiner:** Local aggregation on mapper (mini-reducer)

**Diagram: MapReduce**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      MAPREDUCE PATTERN                        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                   MAP PHASE                            │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Input Split 1 ──▶ Mapper 1 ──▶ (k1,v1) (k2,v2)    │    │
│  │  Input Split 2 ──▶ Mapper 2 ──▶ (k1,v3) (k3,v4)    │    │
│  │  Input Split 3 ──▶ Mapper 3 ──▶ (k2,v5) (k3,v6)    │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                            │                                 │
│                            ▼                                 │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                   SHUFFLE PHASE                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Sort and group by key:                             │    │
│  │  k1: [v1, v3]                                      │    │
│  │  k2: [v2, v5]                                      │    │
│  │  k3: [v4, v6]                                      │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                            │                                 │
│                            ▼                                 │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                   REDUCE PHASE                         │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Reducer 1: k1: [v1,v3] ──▶ Result 1               │    │
│  │  Reducer 2: k2: [v2,v5] ──▶ Result 2               │    │
│  │  Reducer 3: k3: [v4,v6] ──▶ Result 3               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                            │                                 │
│                            ▼                                 │
│                    ┌──────────────┐                         │
│                    │ Final Output │                         │
│                    └──────────────┘                         │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 WORD COUNT EXAMPLE                     │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Input: "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"│    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Map: emit (word, 1) for each word                 │    │
│  │    (the,1) (quick,1) (brown,1) (fox,1) (jumps,1)   │    │
│  │    (over,1) (the,1) (lazy,1) (dog,1)               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Shuffle: group by word                             │    │
│  │    the: [1,1]                                       │    │
│  │    quick: [1]                                       │    │
│  │    brown: [1]                                       │    │
│  │    ...                                              │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Reduce: sum counts                                 │    │
│  │    (the,2) (quick,1) (brown,1) (fox,1) (jumps,1)   │    │
│  │    (over,1) (lazy,1) (dog,1)                       │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example (Simplified)**

```java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.stream.*;

// 1. Simple In-Memory MapReduce Framework
class MapReduce<MapKey, MapValue, ReduceKey, ReduceValue> {
    private final int numMappers;
    private final int numReducers;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    
    public MapReduce(int numMappers, int numReducers) {
        this.numMappers = numMappers;
        this.numReducers = numReducers;
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(numMappers + numReducers);
    }
    
    public List<Pair<ReduceKey, ReduceValue>> execute(
            List<?> input,
            Mapper<MapKey, MapValue> mapper,
            Reducer<ReduceKey, MapKey, MapValue, ReduceValue> reducer) 
            throws InterruptedException, ExecutionException {
        
        // 1. Split input into chunks
        List<List<?>> splits = splitInput(input, numMappers);
        
        // 2. Map phase
        List<Future<List<Pair<MapKey, MapValue>>>> mapFutures = new ArrayList<>();
        for (List<?> split : splits) {
            mapFutures.add(executor.submit(() -> mapper.map(split)));
        }
        
        // Collect all map outputs
        List<Pair<MapKey, MapValue>> mapOutputs = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Future<List<Pair<MapKey, MapValue>>> future : mapFutures) {
            mapOutputs.addAll(future.get());
        }
        
        // 3. Shuffle phase (group by key)
        Map<ReduceKey, List<MapValue>> grouped = groupByKey(mapOutputs, reducer);
        
        // 4. Reduce phase
        List<Future<Pair<ReduceKey, ReduceValue>>> reduceFutures = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Map.Entry<ReduceKey, List<MapValue>> entry : grouped.entrySet()) {
            reduceFutures.add(executor.submit(() -> 
                reducer.reduce(entry.getKey(), entry.getValue())));
        }
        
        // Collect results
        List<Pair<ReduceKey, ReduceValue>> results = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Future<Pair<ReduceKey, ReduceValue>> future : reduceFutures) {
            results.add(future.get());
        }
        
        return results;
    }
    
    private List<List<?>> splitInput(List<?> input, int numSplits) {
        List<List<?>> splits = new ArrayList<>();
        int chunkSize = (int) Math.ceil((double) input.size() / numSplits);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < input.size(); i += chunkSize) {
            int end = Math.min(i + chunkSize, input.size());
            splits.add(input.subList(i, end));
        }
        return splits;
    }
    
    private Map<ReduceKey, List<MapValue>> groupByKey(
            List<Pair<MapKey, MapValue>> mapOutputs,
            Reducer<ReduceKey, MapKey, MapValue, ReduceValue> reducer) {
        
        Map<ReduceKey, List<MapValue>> grouped = new HashMap<>();
        
        for (Pair<MapKey, MapValue> pair : mapOutputs) {
            ReduceKey key = reducer.getReduceKey(pair.key);
            grouped.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new ArrayList<>())
                   .add(pair.value);
        }
        
        return grouped;
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
    
    // Helper classes
    static class Pair<K, V> {
        K key;
        V value;
        Pair(K key, V value) { this.key = key; this.value = value; }
    }
    
    interface Mapper<K, V> {
        List<Pair<K, V>> map(List<?> input);
    }
    
    interface Reducer<ReduceKey, MapKey, MapValue, ReduceValue> {
        ReduceKey getReduceKey(MapKey mapKey);
        Pair<ReduceKey, ReduceValue> reduce(ReduceKey key, List<MapValue> values);
    }
}

// 2. Word Count Example
class WordCount {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        // Create input data
        List<String> documents = Arrays.asList(
            "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog",
            "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog",
            "hello world hello world",
            "map reduce is great"
        );
        
        // Create MapReduce instance
        MapReduce<String, Integer, String, Integer> mr = 
            new MapReduce<>(2, 2);
        
        // Define mapper
        MapReduce.Mapper<String, Integer> mapper = input -> {
            List<MapReduce.Pair<String, Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
            for (Object item : input) {
                String doc = (String) item;
                for (String word : doc.split("\\s+")) {
                    result.add(new MapReduce.Pair<>(word, 1));
                }
            }
            return result;
        };
        
        // Define reducer
        MapReduce.Reducer<String, String, Integer, Integer> reducer = 
            new MapReduce.Reducer<>() {
                public String getReduceKey(String mapKey) {
                    return mapKey; // same key
                }
                
                public MapReduce.Pair<String, Integer> reduce(
                        String key, List<Integer> values) {
                    int sum = values.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).sum();
                    return new MapReduce.Pair<>(key, sum);
                }
				
        };
        
        // Execute MapReduce
        List<MapReduce.Pair<String, Integer>> results = 
            mr.execute(documents, mapper, reducer);
        
        // Print results
        results.sort((a, b) -> b.value - a.value);
        for (MapReduce.Pair<String, Integer> pair : results) {
            System.out.println(pair.key + ": " + pair.value);
        }
        
        mr.shutdown();
    }
}

// 3. Parallel Stream Implementation (Java 8+)
class StreamMapReduce {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> documents = Arrays.asList(
            "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog",
            "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog",
            "hello world hello world",
            "map reduce is great"
        );
        
        // Map phase (parallel)
        Map<String, Integer> wordCounts = documents.parallelStream()
            .flatMap(doc -> Arrays.stream(doc.split("\\s+")))
            .map(word -> new AbstractMap.SimpleEntry<>(word, 1))
            .collect(
                Collectors.groupingByConcurrent(
                    Map.Entry::getKey,
                    Collectors.summingInt(Map.Entry::getValue)
                )
            );
        
        // Print results
        wordCounts.entrySet().stream()
            .sorted((a, b) -> b.getValue() - a.getValue())
            .forEach(e -> System.out.println(e.getKey() + ": " + e.getValue()));
    }
}

// 4. CompletableFuture-based MapReduce
class CompletableFutureMapReduce {
    private final ExecutorService executor = 
        Executors.newFixedThreadPool(10);
    
    public CompletableFuture<Map<String, Long>> processFiles(
            List<Path> files) {
        
        // Map phase: process each file asynchronously
        List<CompletableFuture<Map<String, Long>>> mapFutures = files.stream()
            .map(file -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
                () -> processFile(file), executor))
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
        
        // Combine all map results (reduce phase)
        CompletableFuture<Map<String, Long>> result =
```
