```
│  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐                       │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Check state      │                       │   │    │
│  │  │  └────────┬─────────┘                       │   │    │
│  │  │           │                                 │   │    │
│  │  │           ├─────Valid─────┐                 │   │    │
│  │  │           │               │                 │   │    │
│  │  │           │ Invalid       │                 │   │    │
│  │  │           ▼               ▼                 │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐        │   │    │
│  │  │  │   BALK!      │  │ Execute      │        │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Return false │  │ operation    │        │   │    │
│  │  │  │ or throw     │  │              │        │   │    │
│  │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────┬───────┘        │   │    │
│  │  │                           │                 │   │    │
│  │  │                           ▼                 │   │    │
│  │  │                   ┌──────────────┐         │   │    │
│  │  │                   │ Update state │         │   │    │
│  │  │                   │ (e.g., busy) │         │   │    │
│  │  │                   └──────────────┘         │   │    │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 USE CASE EXAMPLES                      │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Example 1: Starting a Service                      │    │
│  │    if (!isRunning) { start(); }                     │    │
│  │    else { return; } // balk                        │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Example 2: Lazy Initialization                     │    │
│  │    if (instance == null) { create(); }              │    │
│  │    else { return instance; }                        │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Example 3: Task Execution (already busy)          │    │
│  │    if (!isBusy) { processTask(); }                  │    │
│  │    else { return false; } // balk                  │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.*;

// 1. Service that can only be started once
class StartableService {
    private volatile boolean started = false;
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    
    public boolean start() {
        synchronized (lock) {
            if (started) {
                System.out.println("Service already started - balking");
                return false; // Balk
            }
            
            System.out.println("Starting service...");
            // Simulate startup
            try {
                Thread.sleep(100);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                return false;
            }
            started = true;
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    public boolean isStarted() {
        return started;
    }
}

// 2. Task Processor that bails if already busy
class TaskProcessor {
    private final AtomicBoolean busy = new AtomicBoolean(false);
    private final ExecutorService executor = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();
    
    public boolean processTask(Runnable task) {
        // Try to acquire busy flag atomically
        if (!busy.compareAndSet(false, true)) {
            System.out.println("Processor busy - balking");
            return false; // Balk
        }
        
        // Execute task
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                task.run();
            } finally {
                busy.set(false); // Release busy flag
            }
        });
        
        return true;
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 3. Cached Data with Balking Refresh
class DataCache {
    private String data;
    private long lastUpdated;
    private final AtomicBoolean refreshing = new AtomicBoolean(false);
    private final long cacheTimeout = 5000; // 5 seconds
    
    public String getData() {
        long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
        if (data == null || (now - lastUpdated) > cacheTimeout) {
            // Data is stale, try to refresh
            if (refreshing.compareAndSet(false, true)) {
                // Start refresh in background
                refreshData();
            }
            // Return stale data (or null) while refreshing
        }
        return data;
    }
    
    private void refreshData() {
        new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                System.out.println("Refreshing data...");
                Thread.sleep(1000); // Simulate network call
                data = "Fresh data at " + System.currentTimeMillis();
                lastUpdated = System.currentTimeMillis();
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            } finally {
                refreshing.set(false);
            }
        }).start();
    }
}

// 4. File Writer with Balking on Open
class FileWriter {
    private boolean open = false;
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    private String fileName;
    
    public boolean open(String fileName) {
        synchronized (lock) {
            if (open) {
                System.out.println("File already open - balking");
                return false;
            }
            
            this.fileName = fileName;
            System.out.println("Opening file: " + fileName);
            open = true;
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    public boolean write(String content) {
        synchronized (lock) {
            if (!open) {
                System.out.println("File not open - cannot write");
                return false;
            }
            System.out.println("Writing to " + fileName + ": " + content);
            return true;
        }
    }
    
    public void close() {
        synchronized (lock) {
            if (!open) return;
            System.out.println("Closing file: " + fileName);
            open = false;
        }
    }
}

// 5. Usage Example
public class BalkingExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        // Example 1: Startable Service
        System.out.println("=== Startable Service ===");
        StartableService service = new StartableService();
        service.start(); // First call - starts
        service.start(); // Second call - balks
        
        // Example 2: Task Processor
        System.out.println("\n=== Task Processor ===");
        TaskProcessor processor = new TaskProcessor();
        
        // First task - will execute
        processor.processTask(() -> {
            System.out.println("Executing long task...");
            try { Thread.sleep(2000); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}
        });
        
        // Second task - will balk (processor busy)
        boolean accepted = processor.processTask(() -> 
            System.out.println("This task won't run"));
        System.out.println("Task accepted? " + accepted);
        
        // Wait for first task to complete
        Thread.sleep(3000);
        
        // Third task - will execute now
        processor.processTask(() -> System.out.println("Third task runs"));
        
        // Example 3: Data Cache
        System.out.println("\n=== Data Cache ===");
        DataCache cache = new DataCache();
        System.out.println("First get: " + cache.getData());
        System.out.println("Second get: " + cache.getData());
        Thread.sleep(2000);
        System.out.println("After delay: " + cache.getData());
        
        // Example 4: File Writer
        System.out.println("\n=== File Writer ===");
        FileWriter writer = new FileWriter();
        writer.write("test"); // Fails - not open
        writer.open("test.txt");
        writer.write("Hello");
        writer.write("World");
        writer.close();
        writer.write("After close"); // Fails
        
        processor.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Balking Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When an operation should only be performed once (like initialization).

2. When an object can only handle one operation at a time.

3. When waiting would be pointless or dangerous.

4. When you want fail-fast behavior.

5. In resource managers where operations can be rejected.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Service Lifecycle:** Preventing multiple start/stop operations
- **Task Execution:** Rejecting tasks when processor is busy
- **Connection Pools:** Balking when no connections available
- **Cache Refresh:** Only one refresh at a time
- **File Operations:** Preventing concurrent writes

**Advantages of Balking Pattern**

1. **Simplicity:** No waiting, no complex state management.

2. **Fail-Fast:** Immediate feedback to caller.

3. **No Blocking:** Threads never wait.

4. **Resource Protection:** Prevents concurrent operations.

5. **Predictable Behavior:** Clear failure path.

**Disadvantages of Balking Pattern**

1. **May Not Fit:** Some operations must succeed.

2. **Caller Responsibility:** Caller must handle balk.

3. **Retry Logic:** Caller may need to implement retry.

4. **State Consistency:** Must ensure state is correct.

5. **Not Always Appropriate:** For operations that must eventually succeed.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Balking | Return if not ready | No waiting |
| Guarded Suspension | Wait until ready | Blocks |
| Retry | Retry on failure | Automatic retry |
| Circuit Breaker | Stop calling failing | State machine |

**Balking vs Guarded Suspension**

- **Balking:** Returns immediately if condition not met
- **Guarded Suspension:** Waits until condition is met

**Balking vs Retry**

- **Balking:** Returns failure immediately
- **Retry:** Automatically tries again

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Not Using Atomic Check:** Check and action not atomic.

2. **Incorrect State Management:** State may become inconsistent.

3. **No Retry Strategy:** Caller may need retry logic.

4. **Hidden Failures:** Caller may not handle balk.

5. **Race Conditions:** Check-then-act issues.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Atomic Operations:** Compare-and-set for flags.

2. **Document Balking:** Make clear when method balks.

3. **Return Useful Status:** Indicate why operation failed.

4. **Consider Callback:** For async notification.

5. **Use Timeouts:** For operations that can wait.

6. **Test Concurrent Scenarios:** Verify atomic behavior.

---

[Page 32]

## Chapter 30: Thread-Local Storage Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Thread-Local Storage pattern provides each thread with its own copy of a variable, eliminating the need for synchronization. Each thread sees its own independent value.

**Understanding the Problem**

In concurrent programming, shared mutable state is a major source of complexity:

1. **Synchronization Overhead:** Locks add overhead
2. **Deadlock Risk:** Complex lock ordering
3. **Race Conditions:** Hard to debug
4. **Performance:** Contention reduces scalability

Sometimes you need data that is thread-specific:
- Transaction contexts
- User sessions
- Database connections
- Request-scoped data

**The Solution**

Thread-Local Storage provides a variable that appears to be global but actually has separate storage for each thread. Threads can access their own copy without synchronization.

**Key Concepts**

- **Thread-Local Variable:** Separate copy per thread
- **Initial Value:** Default value for new threads
- **Inheritance:** Child threads may inherit parent's values
- **Cleanup:** Resources must be cleaned up

**Diagram: Thread-Local Storage**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 THREAD-LOCAL STORAGE                          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                  THREAD-LOCAL VARIABLE                 │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐         │    │
│  │  │ Thread 1 │  │ Thread 2 │  │ Thread 3 │         │    │
│  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘         │    │
│  │       │             │             │                │    │
│  │       │ reads       │ reads       │ reads          │    │
│  │       ▼             ▼             ▼                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │  │ Copy for │  │ Copy for │  │ Copy for │        │    │
│  │  │ Thread 1 │  │ Thread 2 │  │ Thread 3 │        │    │
│  │  │   "A"    │  │   "B"    │  │   "C"    │        │    │
│  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘        │    │
│  │       │             │             │                │    │
│  │       │ writes      │ writes      │ writes         │    │
│  │       ▼             ▼             ▼                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐        │    │
│  │  │ Copy for │  │ Copy for │  │ Copy for │        │    │
│  │  │ Thread 1 │  │ Thread 2 │  │ Thread 3 │        │    │
│  │  │   "X"    │  │   "Y"    │  │   "Z"    │        │    │
│  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘        │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              JAVA THREADLOCAL EXAMPLE                  │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Simple ThreadLocal                                │    │
│  │  private static ThreadLocal<Integer> threadLocal =   │    │
│  │      ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> 0);               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Transaction context                               │    │
│  │  private static ThreadLocal<Transaction> tx =        │    │
│  │      new ThreadLocal<>();                            │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  // Inheritable ThreadLocal (child threads inherit)  │    │
│  │  private static InheritableThreadLocal<String> ctx = │    │
│  │      new InheritableThreadLocal<>();                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Examples**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.*;

// 1. Basic ThreadLocal
class ThreadLocalExample {
    // Simple ThreadLocal with initial value
    private static final ThreadLocal<Integer> threadLocal = 
        ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> 0);
    
    // ThreadLocal with custom initializer
    private static final ThreadLocal<String> userId = 
        ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> "anonymous");
    
    // Inheritable ThreadLocal (child threads inherit)
    private static final InheritableThreadLocal<String> sessionId = 
        new InheritableThreadLocal<>();
    
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        // Create multiple threads
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            final int threadId = i;
            new Thread(() -> {
                // Each thread has its own copy
                Integer value = threadLocal.get();
                System.out.println("Thread " + threadId + 
                    " initial: " + value);
                
                // Modify thread-local value
                threadLocal.set(value + threadId + 1);
                System.out.println("Thread " + threadId + 
                    " after set: " + threadLocal.get());
                
                // Thread-specific user
                userId.set("user-" + threadId);
                System.out.println("Thread " + threadId + 
                    " userId: " + userId.get());
                
                // Child thread inherits sessionId
                sessionId.set("session-" + threadId);
                new Thread(() -> {
                    System.out.println("Child thread inherits session: " + 
                        sessionId.get());
                }).start();
                
            }).start();
        }
        
        Thread.sleep(1000);
        
        // Clean up (important to avoid memory leaks)
        threadLocal.remove();
        userId.remove();
        sessionId.remove();
    }
}

// 2. Transaction Context Manager
class TransactionManager {
    private static final ThreadLocal<Transaction> currentTransaction = 
        new ThreadLocal<>();
    
    public static void beginTransaction() {
        if (currentTransaction.get() != null) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Transaction already active");
        }
        currentTransaction.set(new Transaction());
    }
    
    public static void commit() {
        Transaction tx = currentTransaction.get();
        if (tx == null) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("No active transaction");
        }
        tx.commit();
        currentTransaction.remove(); // Clean up
    }
    
    public static void rollback() {
        Transaction tx = currentTransaction.get();
        if (tx != null) {
            tx.rollback();
            currentTransaction.remove();
        }
    }
    
    public static Transaction getCurrentTransaction() {
        return currentTransaction.get();
    }
    
    static class Transaction {
        private final long id = counter.incrementAndGet();
        private static final AtomicLong counter = new AtomicLong(0);
        
        void commit() {
            System.out.println("Committing transaction " + id);
        }
        
        void rollback() {
            System.out.println("Rolling back transaction " + id);
        }
    }
}

// 3. Request Context (like in web frameworks)
class RequestContext {
    private static final ThreadLocal<RequestContext> context = 
        new ThreadLocal<>();
    
    private final String requestId;
    private final String userId;
    private final long startTime;
    
    private RequestContext(String requestId, String userId) {
        this.requestId = requestId;
        this.userId = userId;
        this.startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    }
    
    public static RequestContext get() {
        return context.get();
    }
    
    public static void set(RequestContext ctx) {
        context.set(ctx);
    }
    
    public static void clear() {
        context.remove();
    }
    
    public static void startRequest(String requestId, String userId) {
        set(new RequestContext(requestId, userId));
    }
    
    public static void endRequest() {
        RequestContext ctx = get();
        if (ctx != null) {
            long duration = System.currentTimeMillis() - ctx.startTime;
            System.out.println("Request " + ctx.requestId + 
                " took " + duration + "ms");
            clear();
        }
    }
    
    // Getters
    public String getRequestId() { return requestId; }
    public String getUserId() { return userId; }
    public long getStartTime() { return startTime; }
}

// 4. ThreadLocal with ExecutorService
class ThreadLocalExecutor {
    private static final ThreadLocal<String> threadLocal = 
        ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> "default");
    
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);
        
        // Submit tasks that set thread-local values
        for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
            final String value = "task-" + i;
            executor.submit(() -> {
                // Set thread-local value
                threadLocal.set(value);
                
                // Value persists across tasks in same thread
                System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + 
                    " set to: " + value);
                
                // Simulate work
                try { Thread.sleep(100); } catch (Exception e) {}
                
                // Read back
                System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + 
                    " has: " + threadLocal.get());
                
                // IMPORTANT: Clean up to prevent memory leaks
                threadLocal.remove();
            });
        }
        
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 5. Safe ThreadLocal with AutoCloseable
class ThreadLocalCleanup {
    private static final ThreadLocal<Resource> resource = 
        ThreadLocal.withInitial(Resource::new);
    
    public static Resource getResource() {
        return resource.get();
    }
    
    public static void cleanup() {
        Resource res = resource.get();
        if (res != null) {
            res.close();
            resource.remove();
        }
    }
    
    static class Resource implements AutoCloseable {
        private static final AtomicLong counter = new AtomicLong();
        private final long id = counter.incrementAndGet();
        
        Resource() {
            System.out.println("Creating resource " + id);
        }
        
        public void use() {
            System.out.println("Using resource " + id);
        }
        
        @Override
        public void close() {
            System.out.println("Closing resource " + id);
        }
    }
    
    // Usage with try-finally
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            getResource().use();
        } finally {
            cleanup();
        }
    }
}
```

**When to Use Thread-Local Storage**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When each thread needs its own copy of a variable.

2. For per-thread context (user sessions, transactions).

3. When you want to avoid synchronization.

4. For thread-specific resources (connections, formatters).

5. In frameworks that manage request scope.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Web Frameworks:** Request/response context
- **Transaction Management:** Per-thread transaction context
- **Date Formatting:** Thread-unsafe SimpleDateFormat
- **Database Connections:** Per-thread connections
- **Logging Context:** MDC (Mapped Diagnostic Context)

**Advantages of Thread-Local Storage**

1. **No Synchronization:** No locks, no contention.

2. **Thread Safety:** Each thread has its own copy.

3. **Performance:** Fast access, no locking overhead.

4. **Clean Context:** Pass context without parameters.

5. **Resource Management:** Thread-specific resources.

**Disadvantages of Thread-Local Storage**

1. **Memory Leaks:** Values persist if not cleaned up.

2. **Pooled Threads:** Values may leak across tasks.

3. **Debugging Difficulty:** Hard to track values across threads.

4. **Hidden Dependencies:** Coupling through thread-locals.

5. **Not Suitable for Async:** Thread-local doesn't work with async (fiber) concurrency.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Thread-Local Storage | Per-thread data | No synchronization |
| Synchronized | Shared data | Locks required |
| Copy-On-Write | Immutable copies | Different purpose |
| Scoped Value | Java 21+ | Better for async |

**Thread-Local vs ScopedValue (Java 21+)**

Java 21 introduced ScopedValue for async-friendly thread-local:

```java
// ScopedValue is better for structured concurrency
ScopedValue<String> SCOPE = ScopedValue.newInstance();

ScopedValue.runWhere(SCOPE, "value", () -> {
    System.out.println(SCOPE.get()); // "value"
});
```

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Not Removing:** Memory leaks in thread pools.

2. **Inheritance Issues:** Child threads may not inherit.

3. **Async Problems:** Thread-local doesn't work with async.

4. **Testing Difficulty:** Hard to test code with thread-locals.

5. **Overuse:** Using when parameters would be better.

**Best Practices**

1. **Always Clean Up:** Use try-finally with remove().

2. **Use with try-with-resources:** For AutoCloseable resources.

3. **Avoid in Thread Pools:** Clean up before returning thread.

4. **Prefer Parameters:** For simple cases.

5. **Document Usage:** Make clear thread-locals are used.

6. **Test with Multiple Threads:** Verify thread isolation.

---

[Page 33]

## Chapter 31: Scheduler Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Scheduler pattern controls the order in which threads execute by managing a queue of ready threads and selecting the next one to run. It's particularly useful for real-time systems and cooperative multitasking.

**Understanding the Problem**

In many systems, you need to control when tasks execute:

- **Real-time constraints:** Tasks must complete by deadlines
- **Priority inversion:** High-priority tasks blocked by low-priority ones
- **Resource sharing:** Some tasks need exclusive access
- **Periodic tasks:** Need to run at fixed intervals

**The Solution**

The Scheduler pattern provides a central scheduling entity that:

1. Maintains a queue of ready tasks
2. Uses a scheduling policy (FIFO, priority, round-robin)
3. Selects the next task to run
4. Manages preemption and context switching

**Key Concepts**

- **Scheduler:** Decides which thread runs next
- **Task:** Unit of work with priority and deadline
- **Scheduling Policy:** Algorithm for selection
- **Preemptive:** Scheduler can interrupt running tasks
- **Cooperative:** Tasks must yield voluntarily

**Diagram: Scheduler Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      SCHEDULER PATTERN                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                     SCHEDULER                          │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │           READY QUEUE                         │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐        │   │    │
│  │  │  │ Task P1 │ │ Task P2 │ │ Task P3 │        │   │    │
│  │  │  │ (High)  │ │ (Medium)│ │ (Low)   │        │   │    │
│  │  │  └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘        │   │    │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                          │                          │    │
│  │                          │ select next              │    │
│  │                          ▼                          │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────────┐                │    │
│  │              │ Scheduling Policy   │                │    │
│  │              │ • Priority          │                │    │
│  │              │ • Round-robin       │                │    │
│  │              │ • Deadline          │                │    │
│  │              │ • EDF (Earliest)    │                │    │
│  │              └─────────────────────┘                │    │
│  │                          │                          │    │
│  │                          │ dispatch                 │    │
│  │                          ▼                          │    │
│  │              ┌─────────────────────┐                │    │
│  │              │  Running Task       │                │    │
│  │              │  (current)          │                │    │
│  │              └─────────────────────┘                │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │           SCHEDULING POLICIES                          │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  FIFO (FCFS):                                      │    │
│  │    Task A (arrives) ──▶ Task B ──▶ Task C         │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Priority Scheduling:                               │    │
│  │    High Priority ──▶ Medium Priority ──▶ Low       │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Round-Robin:                                       │    │
│  │    Task A ──▶ Task B ──▶ Task C ──▶ Task A ...    │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Earliest Deadline First (EDF):                     │    │
│  │    Task with earliest deadline runs first           │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Rate Monotonic:                                    │    │
│  │    Higher frequency tasks have higher priority      │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong;

// 1. Task class with priority and deadline
class ScheduledTask implements Comparable<ScheduledTask> {
    private static final AtomicLong idGenerator = new AtomicLong();
    private final long id = idGenerator.incrementAndGet();
    private final Runnable task;
    private final int priority; // Higher = more important
    private final long deadline; // Optional deadline (0 = no deadline)
    private final long creationTime;
    
    public ScheduledTask(Runnable task, int priority) {
        this(task, priority, 0);
    }
    
    public ScheduledTask(Runnable task, int priority, long deadline) {
        this.task = task;
        this.priority = priority;
        this.deadline = deadline;
        this.creationTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
    }
    
    public void execute() {
        task.run();
    }
    
    @Override
    public int compareTo(ScheduledTask other) {
        // Higher priority first
        int priorityCompare = Integer.compare(other.priority, this.priority);
        if (priorityCompare != 0) return priorityCompare;
        
        // If deadlines exist, earlier deadline first
        if (this.deadline > 0 && other.deadline > 0) {
            return Long.compare(this.deadline, other.deadline);
        }
        
        // Otherwise FIFO
        return Long.compare(this.creationTime, other.creationTime);
    }
    
    public long getId() { return id; }
    public int getPriority() { return priority; }
    public long getDeadline() { return deadline; }
}

// 2. Priority Scheduler
class PriorityScheduler {
    private final PriorityBlockingQueue<ScheduledTask> readyQueue;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    private ScheduledTask currentTask;
    
    public PriorityScheduler(int threadCount) {
        this.readyQueue = new PriorityBlockingQueue<>();
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadCount);
        startScheduler();
    }
    
    public void schedule(Runnable task, int priority) {
        readyQueue.offer(new ScheduledTask(task, priority));
    }
    
    public void scheduleWithDeadline(Runnable task, int priority, long deadline) {
        readyQueue.offer(new ScheduledTask(task, priority, deadline));
    }
    
    private void startScheduler() {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            while (running) {
                try {
                    // Take next task (blocks if none)
                    currentTask = readyQueue.take();
                    
                    // Execute task
                    currentTask.execute();
                    
                    System.out.println("Completed task " + currentTask.getId() + 
                        " (priority: " + currentTask.getPriority() + ")");
                    
                    currentTask = null;
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                    break;
                }
            }
        });
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        executor.shutdown();
    }
    
    public int getQueueSize() {
        return readyQueue.size();
    }
}

// 3. Round-Robin Scheduler
class RoundRobinScheduler {
    private final Queue<Runnable>[] priorityQueues;
    private final int priorities;
    private int currentPriority = 0;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    public RoundRobinScheduler(int priorityLevels, int threadCount) {
        this.priorities = priorityLevels;
        this.priorityQueues = new Queue[priorityLevels];
        for (int i = 0; i < priorityLevels; i++) {
            priorityQueues[i] = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();
        }
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadCount);
        startScheduler();
    }
    
    public void schedule(Runnable task, int priority) {
        if (priority >= priorities || priority < 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Invalid priority");
        }
        priorityQueues[priority].offer(task);
    }
    
    private void startScheduler() {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            while (running) {
                // Round-robin through priorities
                for (int i = 0; i < priorities && running; i++) {
                    int queueIndex = (currentPriority + i) % priorities;
                    Runnable task = priorityQueues[queueIndex].poll();
                    if (task != null) {
                        task.run();
                        currentPriority = (queueIndex + 1) % priorities;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                
                // If no tasks, yield
                Thread.yield();
            }
        });
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 4. Time-Slicing Scheduler (Preemptive simulation)
class TimeSlicingScheduler {
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> readyQueue;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private final int timeSlice; // milliseconds
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    public TimeSlicingScheduler(int threadCount, int timeSlice) {
        this.readyQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        this.timeSlice = timeSlice;
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadCount);
        startScheduler();
    }
    
    public void schedule(Runnable task) {
        readyQueue.offer(task);
    }
    
    private void startScheduler() {
        for (int i = 0; i < Runtime.getRuntime().availableProcessors(); i++) {
            executor.submit(() -> {
                while (running) {
                    try {
                        Runnable task = readyQueue.poll(timeSlice, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);
                        if (task != null) {
                            // Simulate time-sliced execution
                            executeWithTimeSlice(task);
                        }
                    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                        Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                        break;
                    }
                }
            });
        }
    }
    
    private void executeWithTimeSlice(Runnable task) {
        // In real preemptive scheduler, this would be handled by OS
        // Here we simulate by running in a separate thread with timeout
        Thread t = new Thread(task);
        t.start();
        try {
            t.join(timeSlice);
            if (t.isAlive()) {
                // Task exceeded time slice - would be preempted
                System.out.println("Task preempted after " + timeSlice + "ms");
                // In real system, would put back in queue
                readyQueue.offer(task);
                t.interrupt();
            }
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
        }
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 5. Deadline Scheduler (EDF - Earliest Deadline First)
class DeadlineScheduler {
    private final PriorityQueue<DeadlineTask> readyQueue;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    
    static class DeadlineTask implements Comparable<DeadlineTask> {
        final Runnable task;
        final long deadline;
        final long id;
        private static AtomicLong counter = new AtomicLong();
        
        DeadlineTask(Runnable task, long deadline) {
            this.task = task;
            this.deadline = deadline;
            this.id = counter.incrementAndGet();
        }
        
        @Override
        public int compareTo(DeadlineTask o) {
            // Earliest deadline first
            return Long.compare(this.deadline, o.deadline);
        }
    }
    
    public DeadlineScheduler(int threadCount) {
        this.readyQueue = new PriorityQueue<>();
        this.executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threadCount);
        startScheduler();
    }
    
    public void schedule(Runnable task, long deadline) {
        synchronized (readyQueue) {
            readyQueue.offer(new DeadlineTask(task, deadline));
            readyQueue.notify();
        }
    }
    
    private void startScheduler() {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            while (running) {
                DeadlineTask task = null;
                synchronized (readyQueue) {
                    while (running && readyQueue.isEmpty()) {
                        try {
                            readyQueue.wait();
                        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                            return;
                        }
                    }
                    task = readyQueue.poll();
                }
                
                if (task != null) {
                    long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
                    if (now > task.deadline) {
                        System.out.println("Deadline missed for task " + task.id);
                    } else {
                        task.task.run();
                        System.out.println("Task " + task.id + " completed before deadline");
                    }
                }
            }
        });
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        running = false;
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 6. Usage Example
public class SchedulerExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        // Priority Scheduler
        System.out.println("=== Priority Scheduler ===");
        PriorityScheduler priorityScheduler = new PriorityScheduler(1);
        
        priorityScheduler.schedule(() -> {
            System.out.println("Low priority task");
        }, 1);
        
        priorityScheduler.schedule(() -> {
            System.out.println("High priority task");
        }, 10);
        
        priorityScheduler.schedule(() -> {
            System.out.println("Medium priority task");
        }, 5);
        
        Thread.sleep(1000);
        priorityScheduler.shutdown();
        
        // Round-Robin Scheduler
        System.out.println("\n=== Round-Robin Scheduler ===");
        RoundRobinScheduler rrScheduler = new RoundRobinScheduler(3, 1);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            final int id = i;
            rrScheduler.schedule(() -> {
                System.out.println("Task " + id + " executing");
            }, id);
        }
        
        Thread.sleep(500);
        rrScheduler.shutdown();
        
        // Deadline Scheduler
        System.out.println("\n=== Deadline Scheduler ===");
        DeadlineScheduler deadlineScheduler = new DeadlineScheduler(1);
        
        long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
        deadlineScheduler.schedule(() -> {
            System.out.println("Task with 2s deadline");
        }, now + 2000);
        
        deadlineScheduler.schedule(() -> {
            System.out.println("Task with 1s deadline");
        }, now + 1000);
        
        Thread.sleep(3000);
        deadlineScheduler.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Scheduler Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When tasks have different priorities.

2. When you need to meet real-time deadlines.

3. For cooperative multitasking systems.

4. When you need fair CPU time distribution.

5. In embedded and real-time systems.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Operating Systems:** Process schedulers
- **Real-Time Systems:** Meeting deadlines
- **Workflow Engines:** Task orchestration
- **Batch Processing:** Job schedulers
- **Game Engines:** Update loops with priority

**Advantages of Scheduler Pattern**

1. **Control:** Fine-grained control over task execution.

2. **Fairness:** Ensure all tasks get CPU time.

3. **Priority:** Important tasks run first.

4. **Deadline Management:** Meet real-time constraints.

5. **Resource Management:** Prevent starvation.

**Disadvantages of Scheduler Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** Scheduler itself adds complexity.

2. **Overhead:** Scheduling decisions take time.

3. **Priority Inversion:** High-priority tasks blocked by low.

4. **Deadline Misses:** Hard to guarantee in non-real-time.

5. **Tuning:** Need to tune parameters.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Scheduler | Task ordering | Central scheduling |
| Thread Pool | Reuse threads | No scheduling logic |
| Executor | Task execution | Simple queue |
| Work Stealing | Load balancing | Dynamic distribution |

**Scheduler vs ExecutorService**

- **Scheduler:** Custom scheduling policy
- **ExecutorService:** Fixed policies (FIFO, priority)

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Priority Inversion:** Low-priority tasks holding locks.

2. **Starvation:** Low-priority tasks never run.

3. **Deadline Misses:** Not meeting deadlines.

4. **Context Switching:** Too many switches reduce throughput.

5. **Tuning Difficulty:** Optimal parameters hard to find.

**Best Practices**

1. **Choose Appropriate Policy:** Match workload characteristics.

2. **Monitor Performance:** Track scheduling overhead.

3. **Avoid Priority Inversion:** Use priority inheritance.

4. **Test Under Load:** Verify behavior with real workloads.

5. **Consider Real-Time:** Use RTOS for hard real-time.

---

## Chapter 32: Supervisor Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Supervisor pattern manages the lifecycle of worker processes or threads, automatically restarting them when they fail. It's a key pattern for building resilient, self-healing systems.

**Understanding the Problem**

In complex systems, failures are inevitable:

- **Process crashes:** Unexpected exceptions
- **Resource exhaustion:** Out of memory, file handles
- **Network issues:** Connection failures
- **Bugs:** Logic errors causing crashes

Without supervision, a single failure can bring down the entire system. Manual intervention for each failure is not feasible.

**The Solution**

The Supervisor pattern monitors workers and takes action when they fail:

1. **Monitoring:** Detects worker failures
2. **Restart Strategy:** Determines what to do (restart, stop, escalate)
3. **Recovery:** Restarts workers with appropriate state
4. **Supervision Tree:** Hierarchical supervision

**Key Concepts**

- **Supervisor:** Monitors and manages workers
- **Worker:** Child process/thread being supervised
- **Restart Strategy:** How to restart (one-for-one, one-for-all)
- **Supervision Tree:** Hierarchy of supervisors
- **Error Propagation:** Failure handling up the tree

**Diagram: Supervisor Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     SUPERVISOR PATTERN                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                SUPERVISION TREE                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │                    ┌─────────────┐                  │    │
│  │                    │ Root        │                  │    │
│  │                    │ Supervisor  │                  │    │
│  │                    └──────┬──────┘                  │    │
│  │                           │                         │    │
│  │           ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐        │    │
│  │           │               │               │        │    │
│  │           ▼               ▼               ▼        │    │
│  │    ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐ │    │
│  │    │ Worker A   │  │ Worker B   │  │ Supervisor │ │    │
│  │    │ (process)  │  │ (process)  │  │    C       │ │    │
│  │    └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └──────┬─────┘ │    │
│  │                                           │       │    │
│  │                                    ┌──────┴──────┐│    │
│  │                                    │             ││    │
│  │                                    ▼             ▼│    │
│  │                              ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐││
│  │                              │Worker C1 │ │Worker C2 │││
│  │                              └──────────┘ └──────────┘││
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              RESTART STRATEGIES                        │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  One-for-One:                                      │    │
│  │    Only failed worker is restarted                  │    │
│  │    ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐    ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐│    │
│  │    │  A  │ │  B  │ │  C  │ -> │  A  │ │  B  │ │  C  ││    │
│  │    └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘    └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘│    │
│  │      ↑ fails                     restarted             │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  One-for-All:                                       │    │
│  │    All workers restarted when one fails              │    │
│  │    ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐    ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐│    │
│  │    │  A  │ │  B  │ │  C  │ -> │  A  │ │  B  │ │  C  ││    │
│  │    └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘    └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘│    │
│  │      ↑ fails                  all restarted          │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Rest for One:                                      │    │
│  │    Restart with backoff to prevent crash loops      │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Example**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.*;
import java.util.*;

// 1. Worker interface
interface Worker extends Runnable {
    String getId();
    boolean isRunning();
    void stop();
}

// 2. Simple Worker Implementation
class SimpleWorker implements Worker {
    private final String id;
    private final Runnable task;
    private volatile boolean running = true;
    private Thread thread;
    private final AtomicInteger failureCount = new AtomicInteger(0);
    
    public SimpleWorker(String id, Runnable task) {
        this.id = id;
        this.task = task;
    }
    
    @Override
    public void run() {
        thread = Thread.currentThread();
        try {
            task.run();
        } catch (Exception e) {
            failureCount.incrementAndGet();
            System.err.println("Worker " + id + " failed: " + e.getMessage());
        } finally {
            running = false;
        }
    }
    
    @Override
    public String getId() { return id; }
    
    @Override
    public boolean isRunning() { return running; }
    
    @Override
    public void stop() {
        running = false;
        if (thread != null) {
            thread.interrupt();
        }
    }
    
    public int getFailureCount() { return failureCount.get(); }
}

// 3. Supervisor with restart strategies
class Supervisor {
    private final Map<String, Worker> workers = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private final RestartStrategy restartStrategy;
    private final int maxRestarts;
    private final long restartWindowMs;
    private final Map<String, List<Long>> restartHistory = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    
    public enum RestartStrategy {
        ONE_FOR_ONE,    // Restart only failed worker
        ONE_FOR_ALL,    // Restart all workers
        PERMANENT,      // Always restart (with backoff)
        TEMPORARY       // Never restart
    }
    
    public Supervisor(RestartStrategy strategy, int maxRestarts, long windowMs) {
        this.restartStrategy = strategy;
        this.maxRestarts = maxRestarts;
        this.restartWindowMs = windowMs;
        this.executor = Executors.newCachedThreadPool();
    }
    
    public void addWorker(Worker worker) {
        workers.put(worker.getId(), worker);
        startWorker(worker);
    }
    
    private void startWorker(Worker worker) {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                worker.run();
            } finally {
                handleWorkerFailure(worker);
            }
        });
    }
    
    private void handleWorkerFailure(Worker worker) {
        String id = worker.getId();
        System.out.println("Worker " + id + " failed. Checking restart policy...");
        
        switch (restartStrategy) {
            case TEMPORARY:
                System.out.println("Worker " + id + " will NOT be restarted");
                workers.remove(id);
                break;
                
            case PERMANENT:
                if (canRestart(id)) {
                    restartWorker(worker);
                } else {
                    System.out.println("Worker " + id + " max restarts exceeded");
                    workers.remove(id);
                }
                break;
                
            case ONE_FOR_ONE:
                if (canRestart(id)) {
                    restartWorker(worker);
                } else {
                    workers.remove(id);
                }
                break;
                
            case ONE_FOR_ALL:
                if (canRestart(id)) {
                    restartAllWorkers();
                } else {
                    System.out.println("Too many restarts, stopping all");
                    stopAllWorkers();
                }
                break;
        }
    }
    
    private boolean canRestart(String workerId) {
        List<Long> history = restartHistory.computeIfAbsent(workerId, 
            k -> new ArrayList<>());
        
        long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
        // Remove old entries
        history.removeIf(t -> (now - t) > restartWindowMs);
        
        if (history.size() >= maxRestarts) {
            return false;
        }
        
        history.add(now);
        return true;
    }
    
    private void restartWorker(Worker worker) {
        System.out.println("Restarting worker " + worker.getId());
        
        // Create new worker (old one is dead)
        SimpleWorker newWorker = new SimpleWorker(worker.getId(), 
            ((SimpleWorker) worker)::run);
        
        workers.put(worker.getId(), newWorker);
        startWorker(newWorker);
    }
    
    private void restartAllWorkers() {
        System.out.println("Restarting all workers");
        Map<String, Worker> copy = new HashMap<>(workers);
        
        for (Worker w : copy.values()) {
            if (w.isRunning()) {
                w.stop();
            }
        }
        
        workers.clear();
        
        for (Worker w : copy.values()) {
            SimpleWorker newWorker = new SimpleWorker(w.getId(), 
                ((SimpleWorker) w)::run);
            workers.put(w.getId(), newWorker);
            startWorker(newWorker);
        }
    }
    
    private void stopAllWorkers() {
        System.out.println("Stopping all workers");
        for (Worker w : workers.values()) {
            w.stop();
        }
        workers.clear();
        executor.shutdown();
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        stopAllWorkers();
    }
    
    public Map<String, Worker> getWorkers() {
        return Collections.unmodifiableMap(workers);
    }
}

// 4. Akka-like Actor Supervisor (simplified)
class ActorSupervisor {
    private final Map<String, Actor> actors = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final ExecutorService executor = Executors.newCachedThreadPool();
    
    interface Actor extends Runnable {
        String getName();
        void onFailure(Throwable t);
        void postRestart();
    }
    
    static class ChildActor implements Actor {
        private final String name;
        private final Runnable behavior;
        
        ChildActor(String name, Runnable behavior) {
            this.name = name;
            this.behavior = behavior;
        }
        
        @Override
        public void run() {
            behavior.run();
        }
        
        @Override
        public String getName() { return name; }
        
        @Override
        public void onFailure(Throwable t) {
            System.err.println("Actor " + name + " failed: " + t.getMessage());
        }
        
        @Override
        public void postRestart() {
            System.out.println("Actor " + name + " restarted");
        }
    }
    
    public void createActor(String name, Runnable behavior) {
        ChildActor actor = new ChildActor(name, behavior);
        actors.put(name, actor);
        startActor(actor);
    }
    
    private void startActor(ChildActor actor) {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                actor.run();
            } catch (Exception e) {
                actor.onFailure(e);
                // Apply supervision strategy
                handleActorFailure(actor);
            }
        });
    }
    
    private void handleActorFailure(ChildActor actor) {
        // Simple one-for-one restart
        System.out.println("Restarting actor " + actor.getName());
        ChildActor newActor = new ChildActor(actor.getName(), actor.behavior);
        actors.put(actor.getName(), newActor);
        newActor.postRestart();
        startActor(newActor);
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 5. Usage Example
public class SupervisorExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        // Flaky worker that fails after a few runs
        AtomicInteger counter = new AtomicInteger(0);
        Runnable flakyTask = () -> {
            int run = counter.incrementAndGet();
            System.out.println("Running task " + run);
            if (run % 3 == 0) {
                throw new RuntimeException("Task " + run + " failed!");
            }
            try {
                Thread.sleep(1000);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        };
        
        // Supervisor with ONE_FOR_ONE strategy
        System.out.println("=== ONE-FOR-ONE SUPERVISOR ===");
        Supervisor oneForOne = new Supervisor(
            Supervisor.RestartStrategy.ONE_FOR_ONE, 
            5, 10000);
        
        SimpleWorker worker1 = new SimpleWorker("worker1", flakyTask);
        SimpleWorker worker2 = new SimpleWorker("worker2", () -> {
            System.out.println("Worker2: steady work");
            try { Thread.sleep(2000); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}
        });
        
        oneForOne.addWorker(worker1);
        oneForOne.addWorker(worker2);
        
        Thread.sleep(8000);
        oneForOne.shutdown();
        
        // Actor Supervisor
        System.out.println("\n=== ACTOR SUPERVISOR ===");
        ActorSupervisor actorSupervisor = new ActorSupervisor();
        
        actorSupervisor.createActor("flaky", () -> {
            for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
                System.out.println("Flaky actor running " + i);
                if (i == 2) {
                    throw new RuntimeException("Flaky actor failed!");
                }
                try { Thread.sleep(500); } catch (Exception e) {}
            }
        });
        
        actorSupervisor.createActor("steady", () -> {
            for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
                System.out.println("Steady actor working");
                try { Thread.sleep(500); } catch (Exception e) {}
            }
        });
        
        Thread.sleep(5000);
        actorSupervisor.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Supervisor Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When building resilient, self-healing systems.

2. For managing long-running processes.

3. In actor-based systems (Akka, Erlang).

4. When failures should be handled automatically.

5. For microservices that need restart on failure.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Erlang/OTP:** Supervision trees
- **Akka:** Actor supervision
- **Kubernetes:** Pod restarts
- **Systemd:** Service supervision
- **Process Managers:** PM2, supervisord

**Advantages of Supervisor Pattern**

1. **Self-Healing:** Automatic recovery from failures.

2. **Fault Isolation:** Failures contained to workers.

3. **Structured Recovery:** Clear restart strategies.

4. **Hierarchical:** Complex failure handling.

5. **Observability:** Clear failure history.

**Disadvantages of Supervisor Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** Additional layer to manage.

2. **State Loss:** Workers may lose state on restart.

3. **Crash Loops:** Continuous restarts if underlying issue persists.

4. **Debugging:** Hard to debug across restarts.

5. **Overhead:** Monitoring adds overhead.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Supervisor | Failure recovery | Restarts workers |
| Circuit Breaker | Stop failing calls | Prevents calls |
| Retry | Retry operations | Transient failures |
| Health Check | Detect failures | Monitoring |

**Supervisor vs Circuit Breaker**

- **Supervisor:** Restarts workers on failure
- **Circuit Breaker:** Prevents calls to failing service

**Supervisor vs Health Check**

- **Supervisor:** Takes action on failure
- **Health Check:** Only detects failure

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Crash Loops:** Unlimited restarts without backoff.

2. **State Loss:** Not persisting critical state.

3. **Over-supervision:** Supervising non-fault-tolerant components.

4. **Restart Storm:** Many restarts overwhelming system.

5. **Circular Dependencies:** Supervisors depending on each other.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Backoff:** Exponential backoff for restarts.

2. **Limit Restarts:** Maximum restarts within time window.

3. **Persist State:** Store state externally when needed.

4. **Separate Concerns:** Only supervise fault-prone components.

5. **Monitor Restarts:** Alert on frequent restarts.

6. **Test Failure Scenarios:** Verify recovery behavior.

[Page 35]

## Chapter 33: Rate Limiting Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Rate Limiting pattern controls the rate at which operations are performed to prevent system overload, ensure fair resource distribution, and protect against abuse.

**Understanding the Problem**

In distributed systems, uncontrolled request rates can cause:

1. **Resource Exhaustion:** Overwhelming databases, services
2. **Denial of Service:** Malicious or accidental overload
3. **Cost Spikes:** Cloud costs based on usage
4. **Unfairness:** Some clients consuming all resources
5. **Cascading Failures:** Downstream services overwhelmed

**The Solution**

The Rate Limiting pattern limits the number of operations within a time window:

1. **Leaky Bucket:** Requests processed at fixed rate
2. **Token Bucket:** Tokens replenished at fixed rate
3. **Fixed Window:** Count per time window
4. **Sliding Window:** Rolling time window

**Key Concepts**

- **Rate:** Operations per time unit (e.g., 100 requests/second)
- **Burst Capacity:** Maximum allowed in a short burst
- **Queue:** Temporary storage for excess requests
- **Throttling:** Rejecting or delaying requests
- **Backpressure:** Signaling to slow down senders

**Diagram: Rate Limiting Algorithms**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   RATE LIMITING PATTERN                        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              TOKEN BUCKET ALGORITHM                    │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
│  │  │              TOKEN BUCKET                      │   │    │
│  │  │  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐              │   │    │
│  │  │  │ T │ │ T │ │ T │ │ T │ │ T │              │   │    │
│  │  │  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘              │   │    │
│  │  │     capacity: 5                               │   │    │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  ┌──────────┐                                     │    │
│  │  │ Request  │───▶ Take token? ──Yes──▶ Process   │    │
│  │  └──────────┘         │                           │    │
│  │                       │ No                        │    │
│  │                       ▼                           │    │
│  │                  ┌──────────┐                     │    │
│  │                  │  Reject  │                     │    │
│  │                  └──────────┘                     │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Tokens replenished at fixed rate (e.g., 1/sec)    │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              FIXED WINDOW COUNTER                     │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Count: 3/10                                        │    │
│  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐     │    │
│  │  │  Window 1     Window 2     Window 3       │     │    │
│  │  │  ┌─────┐      ┌─────┐      ┌─────┐       │     │    │
│  │  │  │ 3/10│      │ 8/10│      │ 2/10│       │     │    │
│  │  │  └─────┘      └─────┘      └─────┘       │     │    │
│  │  │     │            │            │          │     │    │
│  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────┘     │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Examples**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.*;

// 1. Token Bucket Rate Limiter
class TokenBucket {
    private final long capacity;
    private final long refillRate; // tokens per second
    private final AtomicLong availableTokens;
    private final AtomicLong lastRefillTime;
    
    public TokenBucket(long capacity, long refillRate) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        this.refillRate = refillRate;
        this.availableTokens = new AtomicLong(capacity);
        this.lastRefillTime = new AtomicLong(System.nanoTime());
    }
    
    public boolean tryAcquire() {
        refill();
        long current = availableTokens.get();
        while (current > 0 && !availableTokens.compareAndSet(current, current - 1)) {
            current = availableTokens.get();
        }
        return current > 0;
    }
    
    private void refill() {
        long now = System.nanoTime();
        long lastRefill = lastRefillTime.get();
        long elapsed = now - lastRefill;
        
        if (elapsed > 0) {
            long tokensToAdd = elapsed * refillRate / 1_000_000_000L;
            if (tokensToAdd > 0) {
                long newTokens = Math.min(capacity, 
                    availableTokens.get() + tokensToAdd);
                availableTokens.set(newTokens);
                lastRefillTime.set(now);
            }
        }
    }
}

// 2. Leaky Bucket Rate Limiter
class LeakyBucket {
    private final long capacity;
    private final long leakRate; // tokens per second
    private final AtomicLong waterLevel;
    private final AtomicLong lastLeakTime;
    
    public LeakyBucket(long capacity, long leakRate) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        this.leakRate = leakRate;
        this.waterLevel = new AtomicLong(0);
        this.lastLeakTime = new AtomicLong(System.nanoTime());
    }
    
    public boolean tryAcquire() {
        leak();
        long current = waterLevel.get();
        if (current >= capacity) {
            return false;
        }
        return waterLevel.compareAndSet(current, current + 1);
    }
    
    private void leak() {
        long now = System.nanoTime();
        long lastLeak = lastLeakTime.get();
        long elapsed = now - lastLeak;
        
        if (elapsed > 0) {
            long leaked = elapsed * leakRate / 1_000_000_000L;
            if (leaked > 0) {
                long newLevel = Math.max(0, waterLevel.get() - leaked);
                waterLevel.set(newLevel);
                lastLeakTime.set(now);
            }
        }
    }
}

// 3. Fixed Window Counter
class FixedWindowCounter {
    private final long windowSizeMs;
    private final long maxRequests;
    private final AtomicLong count;
    private final AtomicLong windowStart;
    
    public FixedWindowCounter(long windowSizeMs, long maxRequests) {
        this.windowSizeMs = windowSizeMs;
        this.maxRequests = maxRequests;
        this.count = new AtomicLong(0);
        this.windowStart = new AtomicLong(System.currentTimeMillis());
    }
    
    public boolean tryAcquire() {
        long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
        long start = windowStart.get();
        
        if (now - start >= windowSizeMs) {
            // New window
            if (windowStart.compareAndSet(start, now)) {
                count.set(0);
            }
        }
        
        long current = count.get();
        if (current >= maxRequests) {
            return false;
        }
        return count.compareAndSet(current, current + 1);
    }
}

// 4. Sliding Window Counter (more accurate)
class SlidingWindowCounter {
    private final long windowSizeMs;
    private final long maxRequests;
    private final ConcurrentLinkedQueue<Long> timestamps = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();
    private final AtomicLong count = new AtomicLong(0);
    
    public SlidingWindowCounter(long windowSizeMs, long maxRequests) {
        this.windowSizeMs = windowSizeMs;
        this.maxRequests = maxRequests;
    }
    
    public boolean tryAcquire() {
        long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
        long windowStart = now - windowSizeMs;
        
        // Remove old timestamps
        while (!timestamps.isEmpty() && timestamps.peek() < windowStart) {
            timestamps.poll();
            count.decrementAndGet();
        }
        
        if (count.get() >= maxRequests) {
            return false;
        }
        
        timestamps.offer(now);
        count.incrementAndGet();
        return true;
    }
}

// 5. Distributed Rate Limiter using Redis (simulated)
class DistributedRateLimiter {
    private final String key;
    private final long maxRequests;
    private final long windowSizeMs;
    // In real implementation, would use Redis
    private final Map<String, SlidingWindowCounter> counters = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    
    public DistributedRateLimiter(String key, long maxRequests, long windowSizeMs) {
        this.key = key;
        this.maxRequests = maxRequests;
        this.windowSizeMs = windowSizeMs;
    }
    
    public boolean tryAcquire(String clientId) {
        String clientKey = key + ":" + clientId;
        SlidingWindowCounter counter = counters.computeIfAbsent(clientKey,
            k -> new SlidingWindowCounter(windowSizeMs, maxRequests));
        return counter.tryAcquire();
    }
}

// 6. Rate Limiter with Queue and Backpressure
class RateLimiterWithQueue {
    private final TokenBucket tokenBucket;
    private final BlockingQueue<Runnable> queue;
    private final ExecutorService executor;
    private final int queueSize;
    
    public RateLimiterWithQueue(long rate, long capacity, int queueSize) {
        this.tokenBucket = new TokenBucket(capacity, rate);
        this.queue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(queueSize);
        this.queueSize = queueSize;
        this.executor = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();
        startProcessor();
    }
    
    private void startProcessor() {
        executor.submit(() -> {
            while (true) {
                try {
                    Runnable task = queue.take();
                    if (tokenBucket.tryAcquire()) {
                        task.run();
                    } else {
                        // Wait and retry
                        Thread.sleep(100);
                        queue.offer(task);
                    }
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                    break;
                }
            }
        });
    }
    
    public boolean submit(Runnable task) {
        return queue.offer(task);
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 7. Usage Example
public class RateLimitingExample {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        // Token Bucket Example
        System.out.println("=== Token Bucket Rate Limiter ===");
        TokenBucket tokenBucket = new TokenBucket(5, 1); // 5 tokens, 1 per second
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            boolean allowed = tokenBucket.tryAcquire();
            System.out.println("Request " + i + ": " + 
                (allowed ? "ACCEPTED" : "REJECTED"));
            Thread.sleep(200); // 5 requests per second (1 per 200ms)
        }
        
        // Fixed Window Example
        System.out.println("\n=== Fixed Window Rate Limiter ===");
        FixedWindowCounter window = new FixedWindowCounter(1000, 3);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            boolean allowed = window.tryAcquire();
            System.out.println("Request " + i + ": " + 
                (allowed ? "ACCEPTED" : "REJECTED"));
            Thread.sleep(100);
        }
        
        // Sliding Window Example
        System.out.println("\n=== Sliding Window Rate Limiter ===");
        SlidingWindowCounter sliding = new SlidingWindowCounter(1000, 3);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            boolean allowed = sliding.tryAcquire();
            System.out.println("Request " + i + ": " + 
                (allowed ? "ACCEPTED" : "REJECTED"));
            Thread.sleep(100);
        }
        
        // Rate Limiter with Queue
        System.out.println("\n=== Rate Limiter with Queue ===");
        RateLimiterWithQueue queued = new RateLimiterWithQueue(2, 5, 10);
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 20; i++) {
            final int id = i;
            boolean queued_ = queued.submit(() -> {
                System.out.println("Processing request " + id);
                try { Thread.sleep(100); } catch (Exception e) {}
            });
            System.out.println("Request " + i + " queued: " + queued_);
        }
        
        Thread.sleep(5000);
        queued.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Rate Limiting Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. For API endpoints to prevent abuse.

2. To protect downstream services from overload.

3. For fair resource distribution among tenants.

4. To control costs in cloud environments.

5. For rate-limited external services (Twitter, GitHub APIs).

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **API Gateways:** Rate limiting per client
- **Web Servers:** Prevent DDoS attacks
- **Database Proxies:** Protect databases from high load
- **Message Queues:** Control processing rates
- **Cloud Services:** AWS throttling, API Gateway

**Advantages of Rate Limiting Pattern**

1. **Protection:** Prevents system overload.

2. **Fairness:** Equal resource distribution.

3. **Cost Control:** Limits usage costs.

4. **Stability:** Smooths traffic spikes.

5. **Abuse Prevention:** Mitigates DDoS and brute force.

**Disadvantages of Rate Limiting Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** Need to choose and implement algorithm.

2. **Latency:** Checking rate limits adds overhead.

3. **User Experience:** Legitimate users may be throttled.

4. **Distributed Coordination:** Hard to enforce across nodes.

5. **Tuning:** Finding right limits requires experimentation.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Rate Limiting | Control operation rate | Limits frequency |
| Circuit Breaker | Stop failing calls | Based on errors |
| Bulkhead | Isolate resources | Limits concurrency |
| Backpressure | Slow down producers | Signal to upstream |

**Rate Limiting vs Circuit Breaker**

- **Rate Limiting:** Prevents overload proactively
- **Circuit Breaker:** Reacts to failures

**Rate Limiting vs Backpressure**

- **Rate Limiting:** Centralized control
- **Backpressure:** Distributed control from consumer

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **Single Node Limit:** Distributed systems need coordination.

2. **Burst Handling:** Too strict limits hurt legitimate users.

3. **Window Boundary Bursts:** Fixed window can allow double burst.

4. **Resource Exhaustion:** Rate limiter itself can be bottleneck.

5. **Poor User Feedback:** Users don't know why requests fail.

**Best Practices**

1. **Use Sliding Window:** More accurate than fixed window.

2. **Return Headers:** Include limit, remaining, reset information.

3. **Exponential Backoff:** Client retry with backoff.

4. **Distributed Coordination:** Use Redis for shared state.

5. **Monitor:** Track rate limiting events.

6. **Granular Limits:** Different limits for different clients.

7. **Queue Bursts:** Allow small bursts with queuing.

---
