## Chapter 34: Singleflight Pattern

**Detailed Explanation**

The Singleflight pattern prevents duplicate concurrent operations from being executed multiple times. When multiple threads request the same operation simultaneously, only one executes, and the others wait for the result.

**Understanding the Problem**

Consider a cache miss scenario:

1. Multiple threads simultaneously request a value that's not in cache
2. All threads go to the database to fetch the value
3. Database gets hammered with duplicate queries
4. Wasted resources, potential overload

Similarly for:
- Expensive computations
- External API calls
- File loads

**The Solution**

The Singleflight pattern ensures that for a given key, only one operation runs at a time. Other requests for the same key wait for the first to complete and share its result.

**Key Concepts**

- **Key:** Identifier for the operation group
- **In-flight:** Operations currently executing
- **Waiters:** Threads waiting for result
- **Deduplication:** Eliminating duplicate concurrent operations

**Diagram: Singleflight Pattern**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     SINGLEFLIGHT PATTERN                       │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                 WITHOUT SINGLEFLIGHT                   │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Thread A ──▶ DB ──▶ Result A                       │    │
│  │  Thread B ──▶ DB ──▶ Result B (duplicate)          │    │
│  │  Thread C ──▶ DB ──▶ Result C (duplicate)          │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  DB handles 3 queries instead of 1                 │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                  WITH SINGLEFLIGHT                    │    │
│  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  Thread A ──▶ ┌─────────────────────────┐           │    │
│  │               │   SINGLEFLIGHT           │           │    │
│  │  Thread B ──▶ │   (key = "user123")      │           │    │
│  │               │   ┌─────────────────┐    │           │    │
│  │  Thread C ──▶ │   │  Executing...   │    │           │    │
│  │               │   └─────────────────┘    │           │    │
│  │               └───────────┬─────────────┘           │    │
│  │                           │                         │    │
│  │                           │ single DB query         │    │
│  │                           ▼                         │    │
│  │                      ┌──────────┐                   │    │
│  │                      │    DB     │                   │    │
│  │                      └──────────┘                   │    │
│  │                           │                         │    │
│  │                           │ result                  │    │
│  │                           ▼                         │    │
│  │  Thread A ◀── result ────┼                         │    │
│  │  Thread B ◀── result ────┘                         │    │
│  │  Thread C ◀── result                               │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  │  DB handles only 1 query                           │    │
│  │                                                     │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Implementation Examples**

```java
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.*;
import java.util.*;

// 1. Simple Singleflight Implementation
class SimpleSingleflight {
    private final ConcurrentMap<String, Future<?>> inflight = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final ExecutorService executor = Executors.newCachedThreadPool();
    
    public <T> CompletableFuture<T> doSingle(String key, Callable<T> operation) {
        // Check if operation already in flight
        Future<?> existing = inflight.get(key);
        if (existing != null) {
            // Wait for existing operation to complete
            return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
                try {
                    return (T) existing.get();
                } catch (Exception e) {
                    throw new CompletionException(e);
                }
            });
        }
        
        // Create new future for this operation
        CompletableFuture<T> future = new CompletableFuture<>();
        
        // Try to register as the executing operation
        Future<?> previous = inflight.putIfAbsent(key, future);
        if (previous != null) {
            // Someone else started - wait for them
            return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
                try {
                    return (T) previous.get();
                } catch (Exception e) {
                    throw new CompletionException(e);
                }
            });
        }
        
        // We are the chosen one - execute the operation
        executor.submit(() -> {
            try {
                T result = operation.call();
                future.complete(result);
                // Propagate result to waiting futures (they're already waiting)
            } catch (Exception e) {
                future.completeExceptionally(e);
            } finally {
                inflight.remove(key, future);
            }
        });
        
        return future;
    }
    
    public void shutdown() {
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}

// 2. Go-style Singleflight Implementation
class SingleflightGroup {
    private final ConcurrentMap<String, Call> calls = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    
    private static class Call {
        final CompletableFuture<Object> future = new CompletableFuture<>();
        final AtomicInteger count = new AtomicInteger(1);
        
        void addWaiter() {
            count.incrementAndGet();
        }
        
        int getCount() {
            return count.get();
        }
    }
    
    public <T> T doSingle(String key, Callable<T> operation) throws Exception {
        Call call = calls.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new Call());
        
        if (call == null) {
            // Already completed or error
            call = calls.get(key);
        }
        
        if (call.getCount() > 1) {
            // Already in flight, add waiter
            call.addWaiter();
        } else {
            // We are the first, execute
            try {
                T result = operation.call();
                call.future.complete(result);
                return result;
            } catch (Exception e) {
                call.future.completeExceptionally(e);
                throw e;
            } finally {
                calls.remove(key);
            }
        }
        
        // Wait for result
        try {
            return (T) call.future.get();
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            throw (Exception) e.getCause();
        }
    }
}

// 3. Singleflight with Cache Integration
class SingleflightCache<K, V> {
    private final ConcurrentMap<K, V> cache = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final ConcurrentMap<K, CompletableFuture<V>> inflight = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final Function<K, V> loader;
    
    public SingleflightCache(Function<K, V> loader) {
        this.loader = loader;
    }
    
    public V get(K key) throws Exception {
        // Check cache first
        V cached = cache.get(key);
        if (cached != null) {
            return cached;
        }
        
        // Check if already loading
        CompletableFuture<V> future = inflight.get(key);
        if (future != null) {
            return future.get();
        }
        
        // Start loading
        CompletableFuture<V> newFuture = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
            try {
                return loader.apply(key);
            } catch (Exception e) {
                throw new CompletionException(e);
            }
        });
        
        // Try to register
        CompletableFuture<V> existing = inflight.putIfAbsent(key, newFuture);
        if (existing != null) {
            return existing.get();
        }
        
        try {
            V result = newFuture.get();
            cache.put(key, result);
            return result;
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            throw (Exception) e.getCause();
        } finally {
            inflight.remove(key);
        }
    }
    
    public void evict(K key) {
        cache.remove(key);
    }
    
    public void clear() {
        cache.clear();
    }
}

// 4. Singleflight with Timeout
class TimeoutSingleflight {
    private final ConcurrentMap<String, Call> calls = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    private final long timeoutMs;
    
    private static class Call {
        final CompletableFuture<Object> future = new CompletableFuture<>();
        final long startTime;
        
        Call() {
            this.startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();
        }
        
        boolean isExpired(long timeoutMs) {
            return System.currentTimeMillis() - startTime > timeoutMs;
        }
    }
    
    public TimeoutSingleflight(long timeoutMs) {
        this.timeoutMs = timeoutMs;
    }
    
    public <T> CompletableFuture<T> doSingle(String key, Callable<T> operation) {
        // Clean expired calls
        cleanExpired();
        
        Call call = calls.get(key);
        if (call != null && !call.isExpired(timeoutMs)) {
            return (CompletableFuture<T>) call.future;
        }
        
        // Create new call
        Call newCall = new Call();
        Call existing = calls.putIfAbsent(key, newCall);
        
        if (existing != null && !existing.isExpired(timeoutMs)) {
            return (CompletableFuture<T>) existing.future;
        }
        
        // Execute operation
        CompletableFuture<T> future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
            try {
                return operation.call();
            } catch (Exception e) {
                throw new CompletionException(e);
            }
        });
        
        // Link to call
        future.whenComplete((result, ex) -> {
            if (ex != null) {
                newCall.future.completeExceptionally(ex);
            } else {
                newCall.future.complete(result);
            }
            calls.remove(key, newCall);
        });
        
        return future;
    }
    
    private void cleanExpired() {
        calls.entrySet().removeIf(entry -> entry.getValue().isExpired(timeoutMs));
    }
}

// 5. Usage Example
public class SingleflightExample {
    private static final AtomicInteger dbCalls = new AtomicInteger(0);
    
    static class Database {
        static String fetchUser(String userId) throws InterruptedException {
            dbCalls.incrementAndGet();
            System.out.println("Executing database query for " + userId);
            Thread.sleep(1000); // Simulate DB latency
            return "User data for " + userId;
        }
    }
    
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        System.out.println("=== Singleflight Example ===");
        
        // Without Singleflight - multiple DB calls
        System.out.println("\nWithout Singleflight:");
        dbCalls.set(0);
        ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);
        List<CompletableFuture<String>> futures = new ArrayList<>();
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            futures.add(CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
                try {
                    return Database.fetchUser("user123");
                } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                    throw new RuntimeException(e);
                }
            }, executor));
        }
        
        CompletableFuture.allOf(futures.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0])).join();
        System.out.println("DB calls made: " + dbCalls.get());
        
        // With Singleflight - only one DB call
        System.out.println("\nWith Singleflight:");
        dbCalls.set(0);
        SimpleSingleflight singleflight = new SimpleSingleflight();
        futures.clear();
        
        for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            futures.add(singleflight.doSingle("user123", () -> 
                Database.fetchUser("user123")));
        }
        
        CompletableFuture.allOf(futures.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0])).join();
        System.out.println("DB calls made: " + dbCalls.get());
        singleflight.shutdown();
        
        // Singleflight with Cache
        System.out.println("\nSingleflight with Cache:");
        dbCalls.set(0);
        SingleflightCache<String, String> cache = new SingleflightCache<>(key -> {
            try {
                return Database.fetchUser(key);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                throw new RuntimeException(e);
            }
        });
        
        // First call - goes to DB
        String result1 = cache.get("user456");
        System.out.println("First call: " + result1);
        
        // Second call - from cache
        String result2 = cache.get("user456");
        System.out.println("Second call: " + result2);
        
        System.out.println("DB calls made: " + dbCalls.get());
        
        executor.shutdown();
    }
}
```

**When to Use Singleflight Pattern**

**Suitable Situations:**

1. When you have cache misses from multiple threads.

2. For expensive operations that should be deduplicated.

3. When calling external APIs with rate limits.

4. For loading shared resources (config, large files).

5. When multiple requests for same data can be collapsed.

**Real-World Use Cases:**

- **Cache Libraries:** Loading caches on miss
- **Database Access:** Reducing duplicate queries
- **API Gateways:** Collapsing identical requests
- **Configuration Loading:** Loading config once
- **File Loading:** Loading large files once

**Advantages of Singleflight Pattern**

1. **Resource Efficiency:** Eliminates duplicate work.

2. **Reduced Load:** Less pressure on databases and APIs.

3. **Consistency:** All waiters get same result.

4. **Fault Tolerance:** If first fails, all fail.

5. **Simplicity:** Transparent to callers.

**Disadvantages of Singleflight Pattern**

1. **Complexity:** Additional infrastructure.

2. **Memory Usage:** Tracking in-flight operations.

3. **Cascading Failures:** One failure affects all waiters.

4. **Timeout Handling:** Need to handle stuck operations.

5. **Distributed Coordination:** Hard across multiple nodes.

**Comparison with Other Patterns**

| Pattern | Purpose | Difference |
|---------|---------|------------|
| Singleflight | Deduplicate concurrent calls | Coalesces in-flight |
| Cache | Store results | Prevents future calls |
| Throttling | Limit rate | Different goal |
| Memoization | Cache function results | For repeated calls |

**Singleflight vs Cache**

- **Singleflight:** Prevents concurrent duplicates
- **Cache:** Prevents future duplicates

**Singleflight vs Throttling**

- **Singleflight:** Deduplicates identical operations
- **Throttling:** Limits rate of operations

**Common Pitfalls**

1. **No Timeout:** Operation can hang forever.

2. **Memory Leaks:** In-flight map grows.

3. **No Cleanup:** Completed calls not removed.

4. **Key Collisions:** Different operations with same key.

5. **Distributed Use:** Doesn't work across nodes.

**Best Practices**

1. **Implement Timeouts:** Prevent hanging.

2. **Clean Up:** Remove completed operations.

3. **Use Weak References:** For cache integration.

4. **Distinguish Keys:** Use unique keys per operation type.

5. **Monitor In-flight:** Track waiting requests.

6. **Handle Errors:** Propagate exceptions correctly.

# PART SEVEN: COMPARISON AND INTERVIEW PREPARATION

## Chapter 35: Pattern Comparison Table

| Pattern | Category | Purpose | When to Use |
|---------|----------|---------|-------------|
| Mutex | Coordination | Exclusive access | Shared data protection |
| Signaling | Coordination | Thread notification | Producer-consumer, wait conditions |
| Rendezvous | Coordination | Two-thread synchronization | Data exchange, meeting points |
| Barrier | Coordination | Multi-thread synchronization | Phase synchronization |
| Double-Checked Locking | Coordination | Optimized lazy init | Singleton, lazy loading |
| Read-Write Lock | Coordination | Shared reads, exclusive writes | Read-heavy data structures |
| Monitor Object | Coordination | Mutex + condition | Complete thread-safe objects |
| Guarded Suspension | Coordination | Wait for condition | Resource pools, blocking queues |
| Thread Pool | Task Distribution | Reuse threads | Many short-lived tasks |
| Worker Pool | Task Distribution | Parallel processing | Batch processing, task queues |
| Active Object | Task Distribution | Async method execution | GUI apps, decoupled systems |
| Future/Promise | Task Distribution | Async result | Async operations, callbacks |
| Generator | Task Distribution | Lazy sequences | Large or infinite sequences |
| Fork-Join | Task Distribution | Divide and conquer | Recursive parallel tasks |
| Leader-Followers | Task Distribution | Event handling | Network servers, event loops |
| Producer-Consumer | Data Flow | Decouple production | Buffering, load leveling |
| Pipeline | Data Flow | Sequential stages | Stream processing, ETL |
| Fan-Out/Fan-In | Data Flow | Parallel independent tasks | Data aggregation, parallel calls |
| MapReduce | Data Flow | Large-scale data | Distributed data processing |
| Reactor | Event Handling | Sync event demux | Network servers |
| Proactor | Event Handling | Async event demux | High-performance I/O |
| Event Loop | Event Handling | Single-thread events | Node.js, GUI frameworks |
| Publish-Subscribe | Event Handling | Decoupled messaging | Event-driven architectures |
| Half-Sync/Half-Async | Event Handling | Bridge sync/async | Web servers, gateways |
| Balking | Advanced | Skip if not ready | Initialization, one-time ops |
| Thread-Local Storage | Advanced | Per-thread data | Request context, connections |
| Scheduler | Advanced | Task ordering | Real-time systems, priorities |
| Supervisor | Advanced | Failure recovery | Resilient systems, actors |
| Rate Limiting | Advanced | Control operation rate | API protection, fairness |
| Singleflight | Advanced | Deduplicate calls | Cache loading, expensive ops |

---

[Page 38]

## Chapter 36: How to Answer Concurrency Questions

**Understanding What Interviewers Look For**

When interviewers ask about concurrency patterns, they want to see:

1. **Understanding of Thread Safety:** Can you identify race conditions?

2. **Synchronization Knowledge:** Do you know when and how to synchronize?

3. **Pattern Selection:** Can you choose the right pattern for the problem?

4. **Trade-off Awareness:** Do you know the downsides of each approach?

5. **Practical Experience:** Can you relate to real-world scenarios?

**The STAR Method for Concurrency Questions**

**S**ituation: Describe the concurrent scenario.
**T**ask: Explain what needs to be synchronized.
**A**pplication: Describe the pattern used.
**R**esult: Explain the benefits and trade-offs.

**Example: Producer-Consumer**

"Situation: In our order processing system, orders came in faster than we could process them. During peak times, we were losing orders.

Task: We needed a buffer between order reception and processing that could handle spikes.

Application: We implemented the Producer-Consumer pattern with a bounded blocking queue. The web thread (producer) puts orders in the queue; worker threads (consumers) take orders and process them.

Result: This smoothed out load spikes, improved throughput, and provided backpressure to the web layer when processing fell behind."

**Common Interview Questions by Pattern**

**Thread Coordination:**
1. "What's the difference between mutex and semaphore?"
2. "How does the Read-Write Lock pattern improve performance?"
3. "When would you use Guarded Suspension vs Balking?"

**Task Distribution:**
4. "Explain the difference between Thread Pool and Fork-Join."
5. "How does the Future pattern help with asynchronous programming?"
6. "What problem does the Active Object pattern solve?"

**Data Flow:**
7. "How does the Pipeline pattern differ from Producer-Consumer?"
8. "When would you use Fan-Out/Fan-In instead of Fork-Join?"
9. "Explain MapReduce in simple terms."

**Event Handling:**
10. "What's the difference between Reactor and Proactor?"
11. "How does Node.js handle concurrency with its event loop?"
12. "When would you use Publish-Subscribe instead of direct calls?"

**Advanced Patterns:**
13. "What problem does Singleflight solve?"
14. "How would you implement rate limiting for an API?"
15. "Explain the Supervisor pattern and its use in resilient systems."

**Scenario Questions:**

16. "You have a cache that multiple threads access. How do you ensure thread safety while maintaining performance?"

17. "Your application needs to process a large dataset. How would you parallelize the work?"

18. "How would you implement a job queue that processes tasks in order but doesn't block the producer?"

19. "Your system is experiencing cascading failures. How would you make it more resilient?"

20. "How would you prevent duplicate database queries when multiple requests miss the cache simultaneously?"

[Page 39]

## Chapter 37: Common Interview Questions with Examples

**1. Q: What is a race condition and how do you prevent it?**

**A:** A race condition occurs when multiple threads access shared data concurrently and the final result depends on the timing of their execution.

Example: Two threads incrementing a counter:
```
counter = 0
Thread A: read counter (0)
Thread B: read counter (0)
Thread A: write 1
Thread B: write 1
Result: 1 (should be 2)
```

Prevention: Use synchronization mechanisms like mutexes, atomic operations, or thread-local storage.

**2. Q: Explain the difference between a mutex and a semaphore.**

**A:** 
- **Mutex:** Binary lock with ownership. Only the thread that locked it can unlock it. Used for mutual exclusion.
- **Semaphore:** Counter with multiple permits. Any thread can release permits. Used for signaling or controlling access to multiple resources.

**3. Q: When would you use a read-write lock instead of a mutex?**

**A:** Use read-write lock when reads are much more frequent than writes. Multiple readers can access simultaneously, while writers get exclusive access. Example: Configuration store that's read thousands of times per second but updated rarely.

**4. Q: What problem does the Producer-Consumer pattern solve?**

**A:** It decouples data production from consumption using a queue. Producers add data; consumers take data. Benefits:
- Handles rate mismatches
- Provides buffering
- Enables parallel processing
- Example: Web server accepting requests (producers) while workers process them (consumers)

**5. Q: Explain the difference between Reactor and Proactor patterns.**

**A:**
- **Reactor:** Synchronous I/O. Notifies when operation can be performed (ready). Thread then performs the operation.
- **Proactor:** Asynchronous I/O. Initiates operation and notifies when complete. OS handles the operation.

**6. Q: How does the Fork-Join pattern work?**

**A:** Divide-and-conquer parallel processing:
1. Fork: Split task into smaller subtasks
2. Recursively fork until tasks are small enough
3. Compute each subtask in parallel
4. Join: Combine results from subtasks
5. Uses work stealing for load balancing

**7. Q: What is the Double-Checked Locking pattern and why was it broken?**

**A:** Optimized lazy initialization:
```
if (instance == null) {
    synchronized(this) {
        if (instance == null) {
            instance = new Object();
        }
    }
}
```

It was broken in early Java due to instruction reordering (constructor could execute after assignment). Fixed with volatile in Java 5+.

**8. Q: Explain the Singleflight pattern with a real-world example.**

**A:** Singleflight prevents duplicate concurrent operations. Example: 100 requests for the same user data miss the cache simultaneously. Without Singleflight: 100 database queries. With Singleflight: 1 database query, all 100 wait for the result.

**9. Q: How would you implement rate limiting for an API?**

**A:** Several algorithms:
- **Token Bucket:** Tokens replenished at fixed rate, each request consumes a token
- **Leaky Bucket:** Requests processed at fixed rate
- **Sliding Window:** Counts requests in rolling window
- **Implementation:** Use Redis for distributed counters, store per client

**10. Q: What's the difference between Guarded Suspension and Balking patterns?**

**A:**
- **Guarded Suspension:** Wait until condition is true (blocks)
- **Balking:** Return immediately if condition not true (non-blocking)

Use Guarded Suspension when operation must succeed; use Balking when operation is optional or can be retried later.

**11. Q: Explain the Thread-Local Storage pattern and when to use it.**

**A:** Thread-Local Storage gives each thread its own copy of a variable, eliminating synchronization. Use for:
- Per-thread contexts (user sessions)
- Thread-unsafe objects (SimpleDateFormat)
- Database connections per thread
- Transaction contexts

**12. Q: How does the Supervisor pattern make systems resilient?**

**A:** Supervisors monitor workers and automatically restart them on failure. Features:
- One-for-one: restart only failed worker
- One-for-all: restart all workers
- Backoff: prevent crash loops
- Supervision hierarchy: failures escalate up

Example: Erlang/OTP, Akka actors.

**13. Q: What problem does the Pipeline pattern solve?**

**A:** Pipeline processes data through sequential stages in parallel. Each stage runs in its own thread. Benefits:
- Multiple items in flight simultaneously
- Higher throughput than sequential
- Example: Image processing (load → resize → filter → save)

**14. Q: Explain the difference between Future and Promise.**

**A:**
- **Future:** Read-only view of async result. Can check if done, wait for result, cancel.
- **Promise:** Writable counterpart. Can complete the future with a result or exception.

Java: CompletableFuture combines both.

**15. Q: How does the Event Loop pattern work in Node.js?**

**A:** Single-threaded loop that processes events:
1. Event queue holds callbacks
2. Loop dequeues and executes one callback
3. During execution, new events are queued
4. I/O operations are non-blocking
5. Good for I/O-bound, not CPU-bound work

---

[Page 40]

# CONCLUSION

**Key Takeaways**

1. **Concurrency is Essential:** Modern applications must handle multiple tasks simultaneously.

2. **Patterns Provide Solutions:** Each pattern solves specific concurrency problems.

3. **No Silver Bullet:** Choose the right pattern based on your needs.

4. **Understand Trade-offs:** Every pattern has advantages and disadvantages.

5. **Start Simple:** Begin with simpler patterns before adding complexity.

6. **Test Thoroughly:** Concurrency bugs are hard to reproduce; test under load.

**Your Interview Preparation Checklist**

- [ ] Understand the problem each pattern solves
- [ ] Can explain each pattern in simple terms
- [ ] Know when to use each pattern
- [ ] Know the disadvantages and trade-offs
- [ ] Can compare similar patterns
- [ ] Have real-world examples ready
- [ ] Can write simple implementations
- [ ] Understand language-specific concurrency constructs

**Final Tips**

1. **Practice Out Loud:** Explain patterns to yourself or a friend.

2. **Draw Diagrams:** Visual explanations show deep understanding.

3. **Be Honest:** If you haven't used a pattern, say so, but explain what you know.

4. **Show Depth:** Mention trade-offs and alternatives.

5. **Stay Calm:** You know the material. Trust your preparation.

**Resources for Further Study**

- **Books:** "Java Concurrency in Practice", "Concurrency in Go"
- **Languages:** Study how concurrency works in Java, Go, Rust, Kotlin
- **Practice:** Implement patterns in toy projects
- **Production Systems:** Study how real systems handle concurrency

Good luck with your interview!

**About This Book**

This book was created specifically for developers preparing for technical interviews on concurrency patterns. Every pattern is explained in clear, simple English with comprehensive examples and diagrams. The goal is not just to help you pass an interview, but to make you a better concurrent programmer.

May your threads always synchronize, your deadlocks be nonexistent, and your interviews successful.
